The nursery keeper demanded the Hush negotiation records. One faceless Root-Mother severed her presence rather than remain named in the argument.

Sivren felt the link beginning to salt through. She could not win a court from a dying cutting, and she would not let Eshara turn collapse into consent.

“Copy the seam to all present witness lines,” she said.

Eshara’s gaze sharpened. “You have no authority.”

“I have standing in the altered memory. Leth has standing through labor custody. Serel carries your own archive cord. Refuse us in words if you mean to refuse.”

Silence would have closed the proceeding without a record. Eshara knew it. Every person in the root chamber knew it.

“I refuse unrestricted copying,” Eshara said at last. “I permit the cut pattern and property count to pass to named witnesses. I do not release the sealed names.”

“Recorded,” Serel said.

The cultivated line bloomed into a web. Cut shape, seal, count, and Eshara’s admission traveled through it. Not the island names. Not enough for restitution. Enough to prevent easy denial.

Nimm cut Sivren free.

The root chamber vanished. She woke on the stern deck with her cheek against wet planks and both arms cramped around the clay trough. Four leaves had fallen. Salt blackened their edges. Leth sat nearby, weeping without sound. Serel had removed her green cord and wrapped it around the surviving stem.

“Did we keep it?” Sivren asked.

Leth touched his sternum. “The count and the cut.”

Serel nodded. “Eshara’s refusal too.”

Nimm poured fresh water over the cutting one careful cup at a time. “Your plant has filed a complaint.”

“It should seek independent counsel.” Sivren tried to rise. The deck slanted, then corrected. “How long?”

“Seventy-three minutes. Longer than sensible roots or sensible people.”

By late afternoon she could hold charcoal again. Hessa brought the coordinate strips to the chart table, moving carefully with one hip against the rail. Together they laid the pressure sequence over the cut pattern copied from Sivren’s own altered memory.

The blank no longer meant absence. It described a route removed with precision: west across the Bone-White Sea, through a corridor where shell ridges opened at low storm pressure, then down along a drowned colonnade.

Sivren inked the course as broken lines. No authoritative stroke. No claim that water must remain where memory placed it.

At dusk, pale fragments crowded the sea from horizon to horizon. Mothwake entered among them under reduced sail. Sivren’s hand shook from the link, but the broken route held. Far below the shells, something vast changed the current against the keel.

Chapter 10 — Across the Bone-White Sea

Nimm caught the missing water in a cup that no one had drunk from.

It stood upside down beside the third cask, its rim ringed with fresh damp. The cask tally claimed ninety-six measures issued since dawn. Nimm counted ninety-seven chalk strokes on the bung board, then found an erased half-stroke beneath the clerk’s thumb.

“Again,” they said.

The clerk swallowed. He had come from a Rainward salt town and still wore a bandage around his hair. “I counted the surgeon’s basin as waste.”

“Water in a basin has not ceased being water.”

“It had blood in it.”

“Then write blood. Do not write nothing.”

Around them, Mothwake drifted without enough wind to stir the sweat on anyone’s neck. Shells covered the Bone-White Sea from horizon to horizon. Small ones clicked against the hull like fingernails. Larger spirals rolled in the oily swell, pale mouths opening toward a sky without clouds.

Nimm took the clerk’s slate and corrected the stores. Sixty-six fit crew required a reduced working issue. Nine severely injured needed full water regardless of work. Twelve surrendered tender sailors had been transferred aboard after their vessel’s lower hold began leaking, and the three Saltjaw outriggers carried shortages of their own. The island pilots refused to reduce their share while rowing watches continued. No arithmetic could make all those truths fit inside seven sound casks.

“Who took the extra measure?” Nimm asked.

“I did.”

“Why?”

The clerk looked toward the sick awning. “My sister has a fever.”

Nimm handed back the slate. “Put her name beside it. You repay by taking the night condensation watch. Theft hides load. Need declares it.”

He stared at them as if reprieve should have felt kinder. Nimm had never found kindness useful when it lied about cost.

On deck, the shell gyre held the convoy in a white bowl. Mothwake’s sails sagged. The captured tender floated two cables astern with pumps working. Three outriggers had taken down their bright Saltjaw cloth to protect it from the salt glare. Every vessel pointed in a different direction while moving nowhere.

Sivren crouched over her broken-line chart beneath a square of shade canvas. The root-link had left a red wound around her wrist. “We need twelve miles west before the shell ridges turn north,” she said. “After that, the deeper water may carry us.”

Talren stood beside her, right arm still bound against his chest. “Oars can give us four miles by nightfall.”

“With sixty-six fit hands?” Nimm asked. “Subtract sail crews, pumps, surgeons, cooks, guards, and anyone you prefer not to kill before we reach the ruins.”

His jaw moved once. Old command habits wanted to turn people into a single number. Rainward had taught him to let the refusal arrive.

“What do you propose?” he asked.

“I propose examining the tools our enemies kindly surrendered.”

The tender’s immersion rack held twenty-four hushglass fittings. Each resembled a thick blue-gray collar with copper lugs and a hollow center. In service, the collars sat around intake pipes and swallowed pressure before it reached an array vessel. Three had cracked during capture. Five carried salt burns that made their surfaces cloudy. Sixteen remained sound enough to ruin someone’s afternoon.

Nimm laid them across the tender’s deck by wear, diameter, and probable appetite. Their own guild had taught them to judge material through its failures. A clean fracture meant poor cooling. A branching white line meant pressure shock. A blue stain around a copper lug meant long immersion near a current the fitting had partially emptied.

Under the eighth collar, beneath Merathi inventory paint, they found a maker’s punch.

Barrow Guild, Third Floating Yard, Kett.

The guild mark consisted of a square hull crossed by three joining pins. Nimm’s grandfather had designed the punch. Nimm had approved its use on fittings sold as harbor-calm dampers eight years ago.

Hessa watched from the hatch without stepping onto the crowded deck. “You knew Kett made components.”

“I knew we made tide dampers for storm piers. I did not know Merath nested them in pressure arrays.”

“Would you have asked if the price had been worse?”

Nimm turned the collar until the punch caught sunlight. “That question has teeth because I know the answer.”

The Barrow Guild had survived lean seasons by treating destination as a merchant’s concern. A fitting met specification. Payment arrived. Workers ate. The ledger closed. Nimm had defended that separation at guild councils where younger hullwrights demanded end-use declarations and older masters called them children trying to interrogate every nail.

Now a Kett-made collar lay inside a captured weapon that had helped bend the Lantern Current, blockade Rainward, and hold a piece of storm like cargo.

“Bring the tender clerk,” Nimm said.

The clerk came under guard, a narrow Merathi woman named Jes Var. She identified six purchase codes from Kett, four from Merathi naval yards, and two built under license in an Ironwake foundry. No culture had kept its hands clean. That fact distributed responsibility. It did not dissolve it.

Nimm made Talren’s deck clerk copy every mark, order number, and measured diameter onto three slates. Hessa witnessed one. Sivren took another. The third remained with Jes Var after she signed it.

“A prisoner keeps evidence?” Talren asked.

“A supplier may accuse us of forging the copy. A Merathi clerk can accuse them with better manners.”

Jes gave Nimm a hard look. “You assume I will.”

“I assume you enjoy being used less than you fear your admirals.”

“Dangerous arithmetic.”

“All arithmetic becomes dangerous when people enter it.”

By noon, two more water measures had gone to the pumps and one injured sailor had stopped breathing. The surgeon covered him with sailcloth. A twelfth death now followed Rainward’s eleven, though the new death belonged to the battle as surely as if it had happened under fire. Nimm changed the injured count from nine to eight and hated the clean subtraction.

The sea remained windless.

Their design began with a broken capstan, three lengths of chain, and the tender’s sixteen sound collars. Nimm arranged the collars around an open copper cage, each mouth facing inward. A sailcloth bladder sat at the center. If the fittings behaved as they did in the immersion racks, they would absorb the still pressure around the shell field. If Nimm rotated half the collars after saturation, the trapped pressure should discharge through the open mouths and pull nearby water west.

“Should,” Talren said.

“An excellent word. It keeps engineers from becoming priests.”

Sivren traced the target line. “The current must stay narrow. A broad draw will pile shells against the ridge and close our route.”

Hessa touched one cloudy collar. “What will it take from the sea besides motion?”

Nimm wished she had asked less precisely. “We do not know. The fittings near Kett made harbor water quiet. Fish avoided them. The tender records show larger arrays interfere with remembered routes.”

“Then a temporary use still carries cost.”

“Yes.”

“Name it in the log.”

Nimm wrote: uncertain harm to shell bed, migratory life, and local oath pressure. No claim of harmless use. Removal after twelve miles or first observed ecological injury.

They tested one collar in a wash tub before committing the full cage. Nimm dropped a weighted orange peel into circling water, submerged the glass, and watched the peel stop against the current while the water continued around it. A second peel drifted normally beyond three handspans. When they lifted the collar, both peels rushed toward its wet rim.

“Localized appetite,” Nimm said.

Jes Var shook her head. “The manuals call it pressure equalization.”

“Manuals are written by people who want nouns to carry their buckets.”

Sivren sketched the test. Leth recorded the change in the water’s direction. Hessa insisted they repeat it with a strip marked by all witnesses, since an unmarked peel could be dismissed as kitchen rubbish. The marked strip stopped at the same distance.

Results gave them a radius and no assurance. Nimm recalculated chain length so the sink would hang below the tender’s pressure vessel but above the shell bed. They assigned a release crew to every joining pin. No crew shared a home polity with the person authorized to order continuation. If Merathi Jes called for more draw, a Saltjaw rower could stop it. If Nimm froze over their guild’s machinery, Serel held the second lever.

The arrangement slowed deployment by an hour. It also meant no single frightened expert could turn necessity into permission.

The crews argued before deployment. One Saltjaw row leader called any Hush mechanism a stolen current. Jes Var said the fittings had saved three Merathi harbors from crownstorms. Leth Osa asked who had been displaced when those protected waters redirected pressure elsewhere. A Kett caulker wanted the Barrow punch filed off before anyone saw it.

Nimm placed the marked collar in the cage’s upper ring where every deck could see it.

“We built part of this,” they said. “We use it now under witness because staying still kills us. If it causes harm, we stop. If we reach Kett, the guild answers in public.”

“Assuming Kett permits the hearing,” the caulker said.

“If Kett refuses, I know which bolts hold the guildhall doors.”

That earned no laugh. Good. The mark did not deserve one.

They lowered the cage at midafternoon. Chains ran from Mothwake’s stern capstan to the outriggers, spreading the load. The copper bladder collapsed as the hushglass entered. Shells within twenty yards stopped clicking. The sudden silence moved across the white water in a perfect circle.

Nimm watched small silver fish turn belly-up, not dead but lost. They ordered the cage raised three feet. The fish righted themselves beyond the circle and fled east.

“First injury observed,” Hessa said.

“Disorientation, temporary.” Nimm logged it. “Sink depth reduced.”

Crew at the capstan walked the bars around. The collars rotated by chain. For one breath nothing happened.

Then the bladder filled.

Pressure snapped its seams outward. Water rushed through the inward mouths and burst west from the cage in a dark lane. Shells spun aside. Mothwake’s stern swung. The first tow line came taut, followed by the second and third.

“Rudder west,” Talren called.

His order traveled to the division leads rather than directly to every hand. One lead refused the first sail angle because it would drag the injured tender across the shell ridge. Talren accepted the correction. Canvas climbed in stages. The convoy began to move.

Two knots felt like flight after a day becalmed.

Nimm stayed beside the capstan with one hand on the hot chain. Every half hour they ordered the cage turned. After each discharge, the dark lane narrowed. Shell banks rose on either side like low snow fields. Ancient reef ribs showed beneath them, wide enough to have belonged to creatures whose names no surviving guild had measured.

At the sixth mile, a crack split the upper marked collar.

Blue quiet spilled across the cage. The captured storm vessel aboard the tender answered with a low metallic cry. Hessa folded around pain at the rail. Nimm hauled the emergency lever, and three joining pins sheared exactly as designed. The upper ring dropped free.

Saltjaw divers recovered it before the shell bed could swallow the guild mark. One diver surfaced with blood running from his nostrils. The water around him held no ripple until he struck it twice with his palm.

“Enough,” Hessa said when she could stand again.

The deep current remained four miles ahead. Their written limit allowed six more. The western sky had begun to redden.

Nimm looked at the injured diver, the stunned fish, and the marked collar dripping blank water onto the deck. “Enough,” they agreed.

They broke the sink apart. Sound returned unevenly. Shells clicked first near the outriggers, then along Mothwake, then around the tender. The convoy kept the momentum it had gained and rowed the remaining miles under divided watches.

Night condensation replenished eleven measures. The clerk’s feverish sister survived. The stores still promised no comfort, but the deeper current reduced rowing demand and put the western route within reach by Day Eighteen.

Nimm sealed the cracked guild-marked collar beside the purchase records. They could have thrown it into the gyre and returned to Kett with an accusation aimed entirely outward. Instead they painted its evidence number across the wrapping in letters large enough to shame a warehouse wall.

At dusk, the white sea opened.

Shells poured over a submerged edge and vanished into blue depth. Ahead, black shapes rose through the calm in two ordered rows. Water streamed from capitals carved as listening faces. One by one, drowned columns breached the surface, lifting a road no chart had admitted into the last red light.

Chapter 11 — The Drowned Parliament

The first column finished rising with a drowned bronze bell clenched between its carved teeth.

Water poured from the stone face. Around it, eleven more columns broke the surface of the Bone-White Sea in two curving rows. Their capitals carried no crowns. Each showed an open mouth, weathered differently, as if the builders had wanted speech represented without granting one speaker dominion over the rest.

Hessa stood at Mothwake’s rail and felt the pressure pattern touch her throat plates.

Not voices. Structure.

The sea around the columns descended in six slow pulses, exposing broad stairs between them. Shells slid down wet stone. At the lowest visible landing, two doorways opened beneath the waterline and breathed bubbles.

“Storm ebb gives us four hours,” Sivren said. She marked the falling line against the nearest capital. “Possibly less. The captured knot keeps changing the interval.”

Nimm tested a sounding line. “Three chambers under the stairs. One hollow, one mostly flooded, one determined to become our funeral.”

Talren had ordered a day’s recovery before any descent. The ruins had declined to remain available for his caution. He stood near the mainmast with his right arm bound and his left hand resting on the hilt of a knife he could not use well one-handed.

“We take one team,” he said. “Fast survey. No removal.”

“One team gives one account,” Hessa replied.

“One team gives fewer people for the sea to keep.”

“Then two small teams, separate lines, separate records. Each may order return.”

His refusal authority had survived Rainward long enough to turn against him. Talren looked toward the nine white mourning strips, then amended his order.

Hessa chose herself, Sivren, and Leth for the upper route. Nimm led Garo and Jes Var through the lower service doorway. Jes knew Merathi array fittings. Garo could hear pressure changes when Hessa’s own hearing distorted. Each team carried a waxed slate, a bell line, three breath bladders, and a weighted release knife. Island pilots Ira and Oru tended the lines from the stair.

“You are bleeding already,” Garo said while Hessa bound her hair.

“Old blood.”

“Your left throat plate is holding uneven.”

“Then record that before you record anything I hear.”

He did, visibly annoyed. A useful witness should occasionally resent the person whose account they constrained.

At the third pulse, Hessa entered the water.

Cold closed over her tusk bands. She descended hand below hand along the guide rope, letting the weights at her belt overcome the air held in her Brakk lungs. Sivren followed with the rubbing roll sealed against her chest. Leth carried the first breath bladder. Above them, the world shrank into a white disk between listening faces.

The submerged stair had been built for walkers in ordinary air. Every seventh tread widened to a speaking platform. Grooves radiated from each platform toward smaller standing places along the walls. Hessa brushed one groove and felt a short pressure answer.

A promise had been spoken here before named parties arranged around the speaker. The pattern distributed outward rather than toward a throne.

She marked the groove on her slate.

At the first landing, Sivren pointed to six shallow cuts in the wall. The cuts interrupted a ring of old script. Crown masons had attacked the inscription with narrow chisels, then polished the wounds and carved new words across them. The later script carried Merathi legal angles. Beneath it, roots of earlier letters remained.

Hessa pressed her fingers around one cut.

The stone answered through water. Obligation. Witness. Renewal. A missing interval after the word for generation.

Pain tightened behind her left ear. She withdrew before the pattern could complete itself inside her bones.

Sivren wrote on her slate: CUTS MATCH ROOT SCAR METHOD.

Hessa answered: SMALLER. OLDER.

They used the first breath bladder beneath an arch. Air tasted of resin and wool. Leth kept his mouth close to the bladder and said, “No root grew here. Whoever cut this learned removal before our archives did.”

“Or taught them,” Sivren said.

Hessa listened to the building rather than the accusation. Below, three sharp bell strokes traveled up Nimm’s line: route sound, continuing. From the ship came one long answer.

The upper doorway opened into a circular chamber. Benches descended toward a dry well at its center, although water filled the room to the ceiling. Copper name plates lined the benches. Many had been levered out. Others carried cuts where names had once continued into statements of standing.

No ruler’s seat occupied the chamber. Seven aisles entered from seven directions. At the end of each aisle, a different water mark showed the height at which its delegation could arrive.

Drowned Parliament, Hessa thought. Not metaphor. Not tomb. A place built for polities who came by separate seas.

Near the southern aisle, a speaking platform carried three repairs from different centuries. The first mason had widened its standing place. The second had added a low rail suited to children or smaller-bodied delegates. A third had cut channels so tide-borne participants could remain immersed while addressing those seated in air. Law had altered the room to fit new parties. Nothing in the older work treated its first shape as sacred.

Leth uncovered voting counters beneath a bench. They differed in material rather than rank: mangrove seed, fired clay, bored shell, black basalt. Each fit the same tally grooves. On a nearby wall, an inscription recorded an island delegation refusing a harbor levy while accepting shared rescue duty. The refusal remained beside the agreement. No later hand had polished it away.

Hessa copied the arrangement onto her slate. Saltjaw courts preserved dissents in spoken recitation, but dominant judges often reduced them to a single phrase. Here the builders had given refusal equal stone. Talren needed to see that. So did every admiral who treated command as a rope pulled from one end.

Sivren found a different lesson. She pointed to route marks beside each aisle, all corrected several times. New channels crossed old ones without erasing them. “A map that admits movement,” she said into the bladder.

“A court that expects parties to change,” Hessa answered.

They had entered seeking a lost clause. The room offered a lost habit: continuity without pretending every generation owed the same answer.

They circled the central well. Its rim displayed a sequence of public obligations: harbor refuge, common pilotage, restraint of levy, repair after wreck, and shared warning of dangerous currents. Each obligation ended in a hooked symbol Hessa had seen only as a damaged tail on old Saltjaw oath stones.

Here, three hooks survived.

Sivren touched the first. A line of bubbles escaped the carving and traveled down rather than up.

Hessa set both palms on the rim.

Pressure opened around her throat. The chamber’s old wording entered as measure, not speech. A promise lived through the named generation. At its end, living parties gathered. They renewed, amended, repaired, or let the obligation return to ordinary water. No descendant entered merely by birth. No territory spoke through a dead ruler’s mouth.

The concept had a compound name in the pre-crown cadence. Hessa separated its halves: release, not abandonment; tide, not severance. A lawful ebb after service, with renewal left to those still alive.

She took the writing slate and formed the words carefully.

RELEASE TIDE.

Sivren read them. Her eyes widened behind the polished shell lenses of her diving mask.

A bell sounded below. Two quick, pause, two quick: evidence found.

Hessa looked toward Nimm’s line, then back to the central well. “We need the full wording,” she said into the second breath bladder.

Leth pointed down the well. Copper glimmered beneath them.

They descended along the inner rim. Ten feet lower, an enormous register plate covered the shaft wall. Columns of names and obligations had been hammered into copper, green with age. A crowned border had been fastened over its upper edge centuries later. Merathi script identified it as the First Crown Register and declared every listed coast hereditary surety for obligations owed to sovereign custody.

The added border covered the old endings.

Sivren wedged a bone spatula beneath one loose corner. Hessa caught her wrist.

“No removal.”

“No discovery without access.”

“Access is not ownership.”

They stared at each other through water. Sivren released the spatula and used it only to lift the edge by the width of a fingernail. Leth slid a mirror of polished shell beneath it.

Reflected marks showed on the dark underside: generation hooks in seven columns, each struck through where the crowned border’s rivets entered. More cuts ran across the main plate. The alteration had converted periodic renewal into permanent territorial inheritance.

Hessa’s lungs began to pull. She motioned upward.

At the breath bladder they debated by fragments, taking turns with the stale air.

“A rubbing,” Sivren said. “Waxed linen. Salt pigment will take both heights.”

“Moving the border risks collapse.”

“Leaving without evidence risks dismissal.”

Leth held the bladder between them. “Copy the exposed face and the lifted corner. Record the limit. We do not need to win every future argument underwater.”

Hessa signaled agreement. It cost her less than admitting he had phrased judgment better than either expert.

Sivren unrolled waxed linen. Leth mixed pale shell powder with oil inside a bladder pouch. Hessa maintained the mirror angle while they pressed linen across the Crown Register plate. The first pass captured the Merathi inheritance clause. The second, narrower strip took the hidden hooks and rivet cuts.

Every movement stirred green corrosion. Visibility closed to an arm’s length. Hessa felt the old pressure cadence building around the central well, disturbed by their contact.

Below, Nimm’s bell line rang three times. Route danger. Return.

Hessa pulled the upper team’s line once, ordering ascent. A return pull answered from the stair crew.

Then Jes Var rang a Merathi engine alarm through the lower line.

Nimm’s team had found machinery.

Hessa swore into the bladder. Sivren sealed the rubbing in its tube. They rose to the chamber and crossed toward the lower service arch, following Nimm’s taut guide rope.

The route descended through a gallery of broken voting stones. At its far end, Jes knelt beside a copper manifold built into the ruin. New Merathi piping had been driven through ancient masonry. The captured tender’s fittings matched the sockets. Crown engineers had visited the Parliament and installed an intake directly over its oldest pressure channel.

Nimm pointed to a stone bar lodged beneath the manifold. Three generation hooks ran along its face. Water hammered around it, seeking the pipe.

“We leave it,” Hessa said.

Jes shook her head and wrote on her slate: CHARGE SET IN WALL.

A demolition cord ran behind the manifold. Merathi forces had prepared the gallery to collapse if the old register chambers opened. The crownstorm ebb had pulled the trigger weight halfway free.

“How long?” Hessa mouthed.

Nimm held up two fingers, then spread both hands. Anywhere from two minutes to ten.

They turned for the stair.

The first charge struck without flame. Water punched the gallery from below. Hessa hit the ceiling, lost her mouthpiece, and swallowed brine. Stone voting tablets lifted around her like thrown cards. Someone caught her belt. Sivren, one-handed, dragged her toward the guide rope.

A second impact tore the manifold loose.

The barred fragment with three hooks spun into open water. For one clear instant Hessa saw the ancient marks blazing white under pressure. Then the pipe inhaled. The fragment vanished down the manifold shaft, deeper than their line could follow.

Release shard, her mind named it, because anything longer could not survive the water in her lungs.

The ceiling came down between the teams.

Leth shoved Hessa through the narrowing gap. Sivren followed. Nimm pushed Garo toward them, but a fallen bench struck the guide rope and snapped it. Black silt erased Jes and Nimm from view.

Hessa found Garo’s arm and kicked upward.

They emerged inside the circular chamber with no lower line. Leth forced the last breath bladder against Hessa’s mouth. She coughed into it, fouling the air, then passed it to Garo. Sivren’s shell lens had cracked. Blood threaded from a cut over her brow.

From beyond the collapse came one bell stroke.

Jes and Nimm had reached an air pocket or retained a bladder. Alive, for the moment.

Hessa answered by striking a voting stone against the floor. One. Then the return signal for rescue delayed.

Pressure surged through the central well. The storm ebb had ended early.

Their ascent became a race against the building filling. Leth went first with the sealed rubbing. Sivren followed on the remaining guide rope. Garo pushed Hessa upward whenever vertigo dragged her sideways. At the final arch, current caught her left ear and turned the world white.

Hands hauled her onto the outer stair.

Hessa vomited seawater between the listening columns. Talren knelt beside her, right arm trapped in its sling and fury making his left hand shake.

“Where are Nimm and Jes?”

“Lower air chamber.” Each word cost a breath. “Gallery collapsed. Alive at last signal.”

Oru counted the exposed stair treads. Water had risen across four in less than a minute. No diver could return against that current with the surviving lines.

Talren ordered wedges, pumps, and every breath bladder prepared for the next ebb. He did not ask whether Hessa would lead. Her body had already answered.

By nightfall on Day Eighteen, only three columns remained above water. The ruins continued sinking until the bell in the first capital touched the sea and gave one low note.

The rubbing survived. Leth opened its tube beneath the chart awning while Hessa lay wrapped in blankets, unable to sit without the horizon tipping. Salt pigment showed the altered Crown Register clause. Beneath its crowned border, the mirror strip preserved three old generation hooks and the wounds where rivets cut them.

Hessa placed her thumb beside the marks. Proof, but incomplete. The release shard had traveled through the Merathi intake into a deeper pressure road. Nimm and Jes remained behind stone.

Throughout Day Nineteen, the coalition sounded the drowned columns. Every six hours, at the lowest pressure dip, Garo struck the bell line they had lowered down the upper shaft. Twice, a faint answer returned: two strokes from Nimm, one from Jes. By evening, the answer weakened.

Rescue proposals covered Talren’s chart table. Saltjaw divers wanted to widen the upper crack by hand. Nimm’s Kett crew proposed driving a flexible tube through the loose shell mortar and pumping air before moving stone. Jes’s surrendered shipmates offered the tender’s pressure valves, though nobody trusted those valves beside another ruin wall. Each plan assigned danger to the people who understood it best and therefore had the most to lose.

Hessa demanded refusal marks beside every task. Two divers withdrew after naming pain behind their eyes. A Kett rigger declined the first tube angle because falling masonry would cut it at the entry point. Talren listened from the end of the table, left hand flattening maps his right could no longer hold. He chose no plan until the people entering the water accepted its sequence.

At the second low-pressure dip, they attempted the tube. Oru carried its bronze mouth down the upper shaft while four sailors fed bladder skin after him. The first fissure rejected it. The second drew three feet, then stopped. From below came a rapid striking that Garo translated as wrong chamber.

Hessa heard no words in the signal. Her damaged balance did not grant insight unavailable to other bodies. She watched the intervals, asked Garo to repeat them, and let Nimm’s own guild crew identify the pattern as hullwright shop code: left passage, air failing, Jes conscious.

They withdrew the tube before the tide turned and cut a new bronze mouth at a steeper angle. Failure consumed six hours and one bladder skin. It also found the passage they needed.

Sivren mapped side fissures. Talren redistributed rowers to pump crews. The Saltjaw divers built a flexible air line from bladder skins. Hessa could do none of the work without falling. She recorded each decision from the deck with her back braced against the mast and refused to let incapacity disguise itself as command.

At dawn on Day Twenty, three answering strokes came from below.

The signal board showed how close the rescue had come to becoming another order issued by the safe to the endangered. Oru had rejected two descents. Serel had stopped the pump when its stitching whitened. A tender sailor had replaced her after naming the manifold design she once maintained. Hessa signed each refusal beside the final plan. If the line succeeded, no commander would later polish those acts into obedience.

Nimm had found another chamber. Jes still lived. A rescue line could reach them at the next deep ebb if the upper stair held. Until then, every vessel in the convoy remained tied to this unmarked reach, with water stores falling and Merathi pursuit somewhere beyond the shell horizon.

The first light struck the rubbing across Hessa’s knees. Crown law filled the broad face in dark, confident letters. Under its edge, the older marks waited in a narrow copied strip. Far beneath the ship, something bearing the same marks moved west through pressure-dark water, beyond every sounding line they owned.

Chapter 12 — Admiral of the Quiet Fleet

Varo put his bare hand into the test basin and waited for the water to forget how to move.

The hushglass lattice beneath his palm darkened from gray to blue. Ripples flattened outward until the copper rim held a disk without reflection. He felt no pain, only an absence of temperature followed by the familiar ache behind his eyes.

“Seven breaths,” Chief Engineer Arel Tonn said.

Varo removed his hand on the sixth.

Water struck the basin walls all at once. Arel caught the slosh in a canvas apron. Around them, technicians aboard Quiet Law recorded intake depth, recovery time, and the slight tremor in Varo’s fingers.

“Array Twelve remains overhungry,” he said.

“Within harbor tolerance.”

“We are not calming a harbor.”

Arel looked past him toward the Armada. Sixty-one warships held three crescent lines across the western approach to Merath. Twenty-two glass tenders moved among them under pale square sails. Eighteen supply ships occupied the protected center, their decks crowded with salt, ash, spare copper, grain, water, and the human labor required to make doctrine float.

Rainward had cut spaces into the formation. Four warships required tow. One tender had been captured. Three supply ships had turned back with storm damage. The official number remained 61 warships because damaged hulls still carried crews and obligations. Varo did not let a clerk erase them into readiness categories.

He had spent the morning crossing those damaged decks. On Quiet Reed, carpenters replaced a split bow brace while two hundred sailors shifted ballast by basket. Its captain requested permission to transfer the wounded and return to line. Varo denied the second request until the brace endured a full-pressure turn. Courage could not make timber sound.

The hospital ship Mercy of Tern held Rainward burns in three sailcloth wards. A young array diver asked Varo whether the blockade had protected anyone. Her hands had blistered inside pressure gloves after she kept a failing intake from drawing a civilian skiff beneath the tender. He could have named the refugee lanes protected elsewhere by Hush. Instead he told her the Rainward blockade had failed its stated purpose and that her act had saved six people within that failure.

At the fourth damaged vessel, deck crews had written the names of missing sailors on the main boom. Officers wanted the writing scrubbed before inspection because some names might belong to deserters. Varo ordered it preserved until each absence received a finding. A state that called every missing person disloyal would soon discover only loyal corpses in its records.

Such inspections fed the version of himself he trusted: engineer, accountable ruler, man unwilling to hide cost. They also fed the apparatus that obeyed him. Every sailor who saw him touch cracked glass might endure one more dangerous test because the Chancellor had shared a smaller danger first. Varo knew the exchange. He used it anyway.

“Test again with a live current before sunset,” he said. “No crew within fifteen yards of the intake mouth. Put the fish screens back.”

“They reduce draw by eight percent.”

“They keep the engineers from learning that fish count only when they clog a pipe.”

Arel bowed without apology. She had argued for removing the screens because seven crownstorms were moving faster than their models allowed. Varo valued her enough to leave the dissent in the technical record.

He crossed the array deck as sailors raised the test basin onto padded blocks. Quiet Law had once carried royal envoys between island courts. Varo had stripped its gilded cabins, widened its ballast channels, and built three hushglass frames through the lower hull. The ship could calm a mile of dangerous wake for eleven minutes. It could also leave migratory shoals circling until they starved if held too long.

No instrument absolved its operator.

Admiral Maraud Sel waited in the chart room with Rainward laid across the main table. She had removed her wet coat and placed it beneath the drip tray rather than letting a steward take it. Forty-four years old, narrow-faced, and precise, she wore no emergency-law jewels. Her left cuff carried a burn where a salt-town fire pot had struck her launch.

“You tested it yourself again,” she said.

“Skin discovers errors reports call tolerances.”

“Your skin commands the state.”

“My signature does. Skin merely complains.”

Maraud slid casualty tablets toward him. Hush losses at Rainward: 143 dead, 219 injured, one tender captured, two salt towns abandoned, blockade failed. Coalition losses remained incomplete. Civilian craft had escaped east through a lane opened when Talren Dask divided his command and allowed local leads to refuse his first route.

Varo read every named dead before he read the tactical report.

Maraud waited. She knew he would not permit summaries to stand in for names, though the Hush apparatus he commanded could strip names from coastal pressure. Contradiction did not become false merely because it hurt.

“Why did the eastern jaw fail?” he asked.

“Captain Erro followed the original closure line after two island pilots warned of a countercurrent. Dask’s port division did not. Their refusal put six light craft where our model expected one command.”

“Did Erro receive discretion?”

“He received authority to adjust for weather.”

“Not for civilian movement.”

“No.”

Varo marked the deficiency against his own operations seal. Rainward had not failed because Maraud lacked discipline. It had failed because the Armada treated uncommanded choices as noise. Dask had turned noise into maneuver.

“We adapt local discretion,” he said.

Maraud’s eyebrow moved. “Into an emergency fleet built to centralize seven storms.”

“Local navigation. Central array timing.”

“People rarely remain inside the categories drawn for them.”

“Then write the limits better than I have.”

She took the chalk, but a signal officer entered before she could begin. His tablet bore a black wax square from the western intelligence cutter.

“Report from the shell gyre,” he said. “Authenticated through two sources.”

Varo broke the wax.

Hessa Kord had reached the Drowned Parliament. Her party had entered during a pressure ebb and removed a rubbing from a register chamber. A structural charge had fired. Two expedition members were briefly trapped and recovered on Day Twenty through an upper fissure. Witness accounts described cut marks beneath a Crown Register border.

Recovered. Varo paused on that word. Intelligence reports often promoted rumor into fact through confident grammar.

“Source?” Maraud asked.

“One surrendered tender sailor released after assisting the rescue,” the officer said. “One shell broker who saw Mothwake depart.”

“Released by Kord?”

“Yes, Admiral. The sailor carried a copied custody statement.”

Varo read the report again. Hessa had released a Merathi crew member with evidence against Merath because a witness needed freedom to speak. It resembled the kind of procedural gesture that changed politics without changing a single ship’s position.

“What does the rubbing show?” he asked.

“Claims of generation marks under the hereditary border. The witness used a term translated as lawful ebb.”

Not proof. Ancient assemblies used generation marks for witness succession, renewal schedules, tax review, and funerary tenure. A cut could indicate alteration or later correction. Hessa Kord knew that. If she still carried the rubbing toward Kett, she believed the ambiguity could survive a public hearing.

Varo dismissed the officer and closed the chart-room shutters.

Maraud remained at the table. “You know the place.”

“I know surveys of it.”

“You ordered the manifold charges.”

“Six years ago, after a crownstorm raised the outer stair beneath a civilian convoy. The ruins redirected pressure into the shipping lane. Engineers installed intakes and collapse safeguards.”

“Safeguards do not usually destroy historical chambers.”

“No. That specification exceeded the order I signed.”

He heard the evasion in his own words. The Tide Chancellery had rewarded engineers for removing pressure hazards quickly. It had classified pre-crown structures as unstable channels. No written instruction needed to say destroy inconvenient evidence. Institutions learned appetite from what their rulers praised.

Maraud touched the black wax. “If Kord is right?”

“Then a register changed.”

“If the change bound later generations?”

“Every sea law binds people who did not write it. Pilot codes bind children not yet born. Harbor walls redirect water for centuries.”

“You answered the legal question with engineering.”

“Because water does not wait for philosophy.”

Neither did grief. It waited for weather.

Eighteen years earlier, Varo had reached Heren Bay after the second surge. Red shells covered the upper road. The house he shared with Ilyan had lost its seaward wall and kept the kitchen shelf, absurdly dry, above twelve feet of water. Cael’s wooden counting frame remained on that shelf. Edren’s blue cup stood beside it.

The old shelter treaty had promised warning bells and upland gates to every listed village. A royal annex, written eighty years after the witnesses died, had removed six outer settlements from maintenance obligations while keeping their levies. Heren paid. The bells failed. A century of denied repair gathered offshore and arrived in a single afternoon.

Varo had spent three days looking for bodies. He found Ilyan in a net loft two miles inland. Cael remained caught beneath a gate that had never opened. Edren did not return.

For years afterward, pressure-memory repeated the original promise across the bay. It used the voices of named witnesses who had never known his family. Petitioners came to hear their ancestors. Officials came to argue jurisdiction. Sailors took tourists along the drowned road at low storm pressure. Memory kept the wound public and did not repair one hinge.

Varo built the first Hush basin because ordinary water should sometimes remain ordinary.

Maraud had known Ilyan. She did not offer comfort by turning his death into agreement.

“Kord will argue that ending clauses were removed,” she said.

“Perhaps they were.”

The admission surprised neither of them.

“Does that change the arrays?”

Varo looked through the stern window. Beyond the Armada, weather towers marked seven distant disturbances. Each tower flew a different sequence of black pennants. Seven crownstorms, drawn by tender lines toward eventual convergence at Hollow Meridian. If they arrived uncontrolled, their combined pressure could close the narrow passage to the outer ocean and drive water back over every Sarravai coast. If Hush arrays met them first, the stored oathwake could be emptied in stages.

The plan carried cruelty. Fishing routes would fail. Reefs would lose memory. Merath would control the quiet corridors because Merath held the glass charts. Varo had negotiated future transfer councils and knew every council could be delayed by a state that liked emergency power.

Yet he had also seen a blue cup waiting on a kitchen shelf while a bay repeated promises to the dead.

“It changes what must be preserved for inquiry,” he said. “It does not slow the storms.”

Maraud nodded once. Agreement on necessity, not absolution.

Day Twenty-Two brought revised projections. Array chiefs gathered aboard Quiet Law with current tables pinned along the mess deck. Arel Tonn presented three schedules.

The slow schedule drew the storms over ninety days and risked two uncontrolled coastal strikes. The middle schedule took fifty-two days and required fixed bases at Kett and the southern reef shelf. The accelerated schedule converged all seven within thirty-eight days. It demanded every major tender, twenty coastal intake stations, and a mobile repair harbor.

“Kett,” Maraud said.

Arel pointed to the Walking Harbor diagrams. Fourteen modular hulls, each independently ballasted, could support the moving array line. Their service docks could split among squadrons. Their civilian capacity allowed injured crews to remain near operations instead of occupying supply decks.

“Kett declared neutrality,” Varo said.

“Kett sold fittings to us and repairs to Dask,” Arel replied. “Its neutrality has invoices.”

“So does every port.”

The slow schedule carried fewer immediate ecological costs. It also put Heren-class strikes on two populated shores. One projection named Saltjaw villages. Another named Veyran nurseries. Varo distrusted models that made coercion look compassionate through colored ink, but he distrusted storms more.

“Accelerated convergence,” he said.

Arel exhaled. Maraud did not move.

“Record objections,” Varo continued.

The western array chief objected to fish-route collapse. A supply master warned of hunger if eighteen supply ships had to support mobile stations. Maraud objected to making Kett a military base against its will. Arel objected to postponing the technical decision until their objections had become ceremonial.

Varo recorded his own: emergency custody could become territorial occupation; Merathi route control must expire after stabilization; evidence from the Parliament must receive an adversarial hearing. He signed beneath the objections and accelerated the plan anyway.

The supply master asked how expiration would be enforced if the sea roads no longer carried the old oaths. Varo ordered public dates stamped into every requisition and placed civilian auditors on the mobile stations. Maraud asked who would compel Merath to honor those dates after victory. No one answered.

Arel turned the projections toward him. “The accelerated draw empties six nursery currents below safe memory density. Sen Veyra approved three.”

“Can the remaining three be bypassed?”

“At the cost of another coastal strike risk.”

“Probability?”

“Fourteen percent.”

Numbers invited a ruler to believe a choice became clean once uncertainty received a fraction. Fourteen percent meant a coastline that might never know it had been endangered or a coastline where families might find boats in trees. Six nursery currents meant damage guaranteed but distributed among lives no projection represented well.

Varo selected bypass on two currents and draw on one. He assigned the western nursery a lower intake threshold because its memory shoots had already suffered storm salt. Arel recorded that the compromise increased aggregate risk while reducing concentrated harm. Neither called it mercy.

After the meeting, Maraud remained beside the empty objection seats. “You could delay until Kord’s rubbing reaches a neutral court.”

“The storms advance while courts establish standing.”

“That answer can excuse any emergency order.”

“Yes.” Varo gathered the tablets himself. “Which does not make this storm imaginary.”

Maraud studied him for a long moment. “You still hear the bay.”

“I remember it.”

“There is a difference.”

He almost said memory had never respected that difference. Instead he asked her to command Kett. She understood the request as trust and burden together.

Sincerity did not soften an order. It only removed ignorance as an excuse.

On Day Twenty-Three, Kett appeared on the operational slate. Intelligence placed Mothwake four to six days from the harbor. Hessa’s rubbing would arrive with it. If Kett’s guild radicals accepted the evidence, fourteen moving hulls could vanish into island channels before the Armada reached them.

Maraud proposed a blockade and negotiated requisition.

Arel proposed disabling three joining engines, then boarding the service docks.

Varo read Kett’s population estimate: 3,420 residents, with at least 780 Rainward and Lantern refugees recently admitted. Four thousand two hundred civilians living on joints, ballast, and mutual trust. Fire among those hulls would become a massacre before any storm touched them.

He drafted the order himself.

Walking Harbor Kett would be seized intact. No fire pots within five hundred yards of civilian hulls. No ramming of habitation sections. Nine marked surrender lanes would remain open throughout approach. Medical craft could cross the blockade after inspection. Maraud would take the service docks and joining engines, preserving all fourteen harbor hulls for mobile array support. Looting carried immediate court-martial. Civil authority would remain with Kett councils except where movement endangered the operation.

Maraud read the final clause twice. “Every movement will endanger the operation once we surround them.”

“Then define danger narrowly.”

“The boarding captains will define it under stones and boiling pitch.”

“Place officers you trust.”

“I trust officers to remain human under stones and boiling pitch.”

Varo met her gaze. “Amend it.”

She changed the clause: only deliberate attack on array or warship permitted movement restraint; civilian separation by itself did not. Better. Still coercion.

“Why intact?” Maraud asked, though the diagrams answered.

“We need the modular hulls.”

“And if they separate?”

“Take the service docks. Hold the southern route. Do not chase habitation hulls into reefs.”

“You are giving them an escape condition.”

“I am giving us a boundary.”

Maraud sealed the order.

Before she left, Varo added a casualty ledger requirement. Every boarding division would report civilian and military harm separately, with names where known and descriptions where names could not be obtained. Unidentified bodies would receive locations, clothing, and recovery witnesses rather than a convenient number. Maraud approved that clause without argument.

“Ledgers do not prevent deaths,” she said.

“No. They prevent us from calling deaths invisible afterward.”

“Sometimes.”

Her qualification stayed in the final margin. Varo sealed it beside his own name. He sent duplicate ledgers to the fleet surgeons and to Kett’s council frequencies before the blockade began, accepting that advance notice might help the harbor organize resistance as well as casualties.

At sunset, signal flags carried it through the Armada. Warships shortened sail and turned east by divisions. Glass tenders followed like pale insects between them. Supply ships opened their guarded center to distribute grapnels, shields, water, and medical chests.

Varo returned to the test basin after the deck cleared. Its water had regained ripples. A small fish moved inside the copper rim, missed by the screen during transfer. He cupped it out and released it over the side.

The fish vanished beneath sixty-one dark reflections as the Quiet Fleet set its course for Walking Harbor Kett.

Chapter 13 — The Siege of Walking Harbor

The first Hush sail appeared behind Nimm’s shoulder while Walking Harbor Kett still argued about opening the gate.

Nimm stood in Mothwake’s bow with a speaking cone and watched their city refuse to recognize its own emergency. Fourteen harbor hulls filled the channel ahead, interlocked in three broad streets of deck and water. Roof gardens climbed the central hulls. Crane arms crossed above workshops. Bright laundry hung between residences with the stubbornness of ordinary life.

Across the entrance, two gate barges had chained themselves together.

“State your authority,” called the gate clerk.

“Nimm Barrow, hullwright, quartermaster, guild member in disputed standing, born on Well Hull.”

“Your authority to bring an armed carrack, three foreign outriggers, and a captured Merathi tender into neutral water.”

“The sixty-one warships arriving behind us.”

The clerk raised a spy tube. Beyond Mothwake, the eastern horizon carried one pale square of sail. Then another broke the heat haze. Then five.

His posture changed before his policy did. “Council order requires disarmament.”

Talren leaned over the bow rail, right arm still in a sling. “We can disarm after you decide whether the ships behind us count as visitors.”

Nimm took the cone again. “Open the north jaw. We have eight severely injured, one trapped-diver survivor with lung damage, and evidence bearing our guild mark. Delay another minute and I will explain gate mechanics to the captain.”

The north gate opened.

Kett received them in bells. Workshop bells called riggers. Market bells cleared bridgeways. The deep joining bell beneath Council Hull sounded fourteen strokes, one for every modular body required to answer. Nimm had heard that sequence during storms, fires, guild strikes, and a fish riot caused by three tons of living silverfin escaping a wholesaler. Never during siege.

Mothwake crossed into the harbor basin. Three Saltjaw outriggers took the western moorings. The leaking tender anchored beside the pumps. Civilians crowded every rail, many of them Rainward refugees who recognized Hush sails before Kett residents did.

Nimm went straight to Council Hull with the cracked fitting under one arm and the Parliament records under the other. Hessa followed with the Crown Register rubbing sealed across her back. Sivren carried the chart case. Jes Var walked unbound between two tender sailors; after the Drowned Parliament rescue, no one had found a useful category between prisoner, witness, and engineer.

The council chamber hung above a joining well where three hulls met. Through grated flooring, Nimm could see black water, copper ballast pipes, and the upper ends of the shear pins that held Kett together. Twelve councilors waited around a table bolted to the deck. Guildmaster Olt Barrow occupied the craft seat. He was Nimm’s elder brother by six years and had perfected the expression of a man asked to apologize for profitable history.

“You brought war home,” Olt said.

“We found part of it stamped with Grandfather’s punch.”

Nimm set the cracked collar on the table. The guild mark faced upward.

No one claimed surprise. That hurt more than denial.

Olt touched the punch. “Harbor dampers. Licensed sale.”

“Installed in array tenders.”

“Modified after delivery.”

“Some. Not all. Jes has purchase codes.”

Jes placed the three copied slates beside the fitting. Hessa named custody. Sivren unrolled enough of the rubbing to show the crowned border and hidden generation hooks. Councilors leaned in despite themselves.

Outside, the joining bell began a continuous toll. Lookouts had counted the approaching fleet.

“We can litigate after we survive,” said Council Speaker Memb Arret, a human sail merchant elected by the resident wards. “Chancellor Senn sent terms yesterday. Medical passage, council authority retained, no burning, requisition payment.”

“Payment for taking our hulls?” Nimm asked.

“Payment keeps taking from becoming plunder.”

“It keeps clerks employed.”

A goblin rigger from South Spoke struck the table. “Separate now.”

Olt turned on her. “Fourteen hulls have not made independent sea in eleven years. Half their joining engines are under maintenance.”

“Whose maintenance deferrals?”

“Everyone enjoyed low guild dues.”

Argument spread by class and ward. Workshop masters feared separation would destroy machinery bolted across seams. Residents of the outer hulls wanted movement before blockade. Merchants favored surrender terms. Refugee delegates knew terms could narrow after gates closed. Three Kett officers insisted neutrality still protected them, as if a word could repel grapnels.

Nimm opened the population ledger.

“Three thousand four hundred twenty residents,” they said. “Seven hundred eighty admitted refugees. Total 4,200 civilians. Fourteen hulls can carry them if ballast, water, kitchens, latrines, and medical stations separate in the right order. If we wait for the service docks to fall, they cannot.”

“Can you do it?” Memb asked.

Nimm looked through the grated floor. Copper pipes shuddered with the common pump. “Not alone. Not cleanly. Yes.”

The council vote split six for conditional surrender, five for separation, one abstention from the refugee seat because its delegate rejected Kett’s authority to decide where refugees would be carried. Outside, Hush warships crossed the harbor’s visible horizon.

A Merathi signal kite rose over the eastern channel. White field, blue glass square, nine black bars for surrender lanes.

Nimm picked up the cracked fitting. “Your vote has not stopped the fleet. My crew will inspect every shear station. Arrest us if you prefer two emergencies.”

Memb did not order guards to move.

By midnight, Nimm had fourteen hull ledgers and no sleep.

Kett’s modular design had grown through centuries of flight, trade, and municipal stubbornness. Each harbor hull carried independent ballast chambers and a shallow sailing keel. Joined, they shared freshwater pipes, waste channels, crane rails, market bridges, fire lines, and three service docks large enough to lift a carrack’s stern. Twelve primary shear pins locked the largest seams. Forty-eight secondary pins controlled flex. Removing them in the wrong sequence could make a hull roll under an uneven crowd.

Nimm named the hulls across the separation board: Council, Well, Loom, Forge, Market, Nursery, Guest, North House, South House, Ropewalk, Granary, Shrine, School, and Mudlark. Heroic songs would have called them fourteen ships. Their ledgers called them kitchens, beds, toilets, pumps, and arguments.

Every civilian received a destination mark. Blue for the fourteen hulls. White for relief craft scheduled through the northern lane. Red cross for medical priority. Black ring for mobility assistance. No mark indicated value. It indicated what the next bridge crew needed to know.

The 780 refugees complicated every assignment. Many families had split across craft during Rainward. Some refused another sea transfer. Others demanded the first white marks because they trusted Kett less than moving water. Nimm gave the refugee delegates control of 780 tokens and required only that each token return with a destination.

Nine escort ships answered Kett’s bell: two harbor cutters, three merchant outriders, the three Saltjaw vessels, and Mothwake. The captured tender could move but not fight. Talren organized the escorts into independent pairs, with one group of three around the slowest hull. No pair had to follow a route its pilots rejected.

At dawn on Day Twenty-Seven, the Hush Armada closed the ring.

Not all sixty-one warships entered Kett waters. Twenty-four showed in the siege line, with six glass tenders and five supply ships behind them. The remaining force controlled outer approaches or continued array operations elsewhere. Maraud Sel’s flagship sent the seizure order in three languages.

Speaker Memb read it aloud from Council Hull. The clauses sounded measured: no fire pots, no ramming habitation, medical passage permitted, local council retained. Then came requisition of all joining engines, service docks, and modular hulls for emergency stabilization.

“An intact prison with payment,” the South Spoke rigger said.

Olt still wanted negotiation. “The anti-burning clauses matter.”

“So do the grapnels under them,” Nimm replied.

Maraud allowed one hour.

Kett used forty minutes to move children from bridge schools, twenty to argue whether movement violated negotiation, and the final ten to strike the first four secondary pins.

The siege began with restraint. Hush engines threw hooked lines rather than stone. Shielded launches advanced on the eastern service dock. Array tenders lowered hushglass curtains that flattened current around the harbor mouths. Kett torsion crews cut grapnels and aimed bolts at masts. Nobody used fire.

Restraint survived eleven minutes.

A Kett bolt struck a tender’s pressure frame. Blue force spread across the water. The tender captain answered with a stone shot at Forge Hull’s joining engine. It missed the engine and smashed through a roof garden sheltering families awaiting transfer.

Nimm reached the garden through dust and bean vines. Eighteen civilians died in the strike. Thirty-one suffered wounds: crushed legs, stone cuts, pressure-deafness, and one child’s broken spine. The casualty clerk wrote each count on a yellow board in the open, visible to defenders and Merathi lookouts.

Dead 18. Injured 31.

Families crowded the yellow board before surgeons had finished carrying the wounded. Nimm assigned two clerks to names and one to descriptions of the unidentified. One dead child carried a blue hull mark blurred by blood. The token number led to a refugee family assigned to Nursery, but their daughter stood alive beside them. The body belonged to a resident who had borrowed the marked coat against the morning chill. Without the coat’s owner present to contradict the token, the ledger would have killed the wrong child twice.

Nimm made the clerk leave the line open. Clothing, age, garden location, two recovery witnesses. No guessed name.

Across the channel, a Merathi signal officer copied the visible totals. That did not return anyone. It prevented the siege line from claiming no civilians had occupied the target. Maraud’s flagship answered with an inquiry flag and suspended stone fire on Forge while its crew reviewed the shot.

Some Kett defenders jeered at the pause. Others used it to carry the wounded. Nimm refused both easy stories: the enemy’s restraint did not erase the strike, and the strike did not erase every person restraining the enemy.

Surgeons converted Loom’s dye room into a ward. Blue runoff stained bandages and made several lesser wounds look mortal. Riggers cut roof gardens into splints. Olt sent guild apprentices to carry bodies, perhaps from guilt, perhaps because the apprentices had strong backs. Motive could wait. Load could not.

Memb stood beside the board with blood on his sleeve. “Separate.”

The council did not vote again.

First sequence: Mudlark from South House. The smallest hull carried repair stores and 186 people. Crews shut the shared waste valve, drained the fire line, severed two market bridges, and shifted sixty tons of water from the exposed ballast chamber. Secondary pins came free in alternating pairs. The primary pin remained until both hull captains signaled level.

A Hush launch reached the seam as Nimm’s crew lifted the pin jack. Merathi boarders could have stormed the deck. Instead their lieutenant ordered shields over civilians crossing the last bridge while demanding surrender. Kett riggers dropped a crane net over his front rank and trapped nine soldiers without killing them.

“Your Chancellor wants the hull intact,” Nimm called down.

The lieutenant struggled under fish rope. “Your people are on it.”

“An insight approaching politics.”

Mudlark’s sail opened. The primary pin rose. For one terrifying breath the hulls clung through suction and tangled weed. Then black water widened between them. Mudlark moved north under its own shallow keel.

A city street had become sea.

Second sequence split Guest and North House. White-marked refugees boarded the captured tender and four relief barges under cover of Saltjaw outriggers. By noon, 717 chose or received transfer north toward the unnamed island corridor. They remained part of Kett’s evacuation ledger until a receiving witness signed for every token.

That left 3,483 people across the harbor hulls before casualties were reconciled. Nimm refused to simplify the number while bodies and duplicate tokens remained under audit.

The Hush line tightened. Glass curtains killed wind along the eastern exit. Talren’s escort pairs pulled separated hulls with tow sails where currents failed. His right arm stayed strapped to his chest; orders passed through six local leads. Twice he proposed a tow angle and accepted refusal from pilots who saw shell shoals his flagship could not.

Third sequence required Loom, Market, and Council to break as a cluster. Their shared bridge carried 402 people because workshop masters had delayed evacuation to move looms. Nimm ordered machinery abandoned.

Olt blocked the pin crew. “Those looms feed eight hundred households.”

“Not if the hull rolls.”

“You came home after years away and decide what Kett can lose?”

“I came home because what we sold followed me.”

Olt’s face closed. Behind him, guildhands waited for a master’s order. Some had built the marked fittings. Others had been apprentices when the contracts passed. None shared equal profit or equal guilt.

Nimm handed the pin lever to a young caulker. “You may refuse me. You may refuse him. Decide whether cloth machinery outweighs the next seven minutes.”

The caulker pulled the lever.

Guildhands joined her one by one. Olt moved last, not converted, not forgiven, but unwilling to let his authority become dead weight. They cut the loom beds from deck bolts and shoved what could be saved aboard Market. The rest stayed across the seam.

A Hush grapnel caught the overloaded bridge.

The pulling launch tried to drag itself close. Instead it drew the bridge sideways just as the first secondary pins released. Four hundred people shifted toward one rail. Nimm heard ballast alarms from Loom.

“Cut the bridge!”

People still occupied it.

The bridge chief, an elf cooper named Sorel, refused. “Thirty seconds.”

“We do not have them.”

“Then make them.”

Sorel opened the emergency side nets and drove the crowd into two kneeling lines. Kett torsion crews shot the grapnel rope. It frayed, held, then snapped. The rebound tore three bridge anchors free. Forty-two people fell between the hulls.

Rescue nets caught fifteen. Twenty-seven died under the rolling bridge or in the closing seam. Forty-four were hauled out injured.

Dead 45. Injured 75.

Sorel survived with a dislocated shoulder and demanded the refusal appear beside the casualties. Nimm wrote it. Obedience might have cut the bridge sooner and killed more. Refusal had saved some and still carried a terrible count.

By night, six hulls moved independently north of Kett. Eight remained connected around the joining well. Hush held the eastern crane pier but had not taken a service dock. Medical launches crossed both lines under Maraud’s order. Kett surgeons treated two Merathi boarders. Merathi surgeons stabilized five bridge survivors. Nobody mistook exchanged bandages for peace.

Day Twenty-Eight began beneath the harbor.

The common ballast intake started hammering before sunrise. Every Hush curtain outside the basin pulled current toward the siege line. Their combined draw reached through Kett’s old channels and found the pressure road under the harbor.

Nimm descended the joining well with Jes Var. Water punched the copper grate in pulses. Something hard struck from below, paused, then struck again.

“Shut intake three,” Nimm called.

The valve crew turned its wheel. Pressure bent the stem without closing it. Jes put her ear to the pipe and recoiled.

“That rhythm came through the Parliament manifold.”

The release shard.

Nimm had last seen it spin beyond Hessa’s reach into black water. The Hush arrays had pulled on the same deep channel for days. Now their siege draw had carried the fragment home to a Kett intake built with fittings from the same trade network.

They could leave it jammed and risk a ballast rupture. They could open the grate and let the pressure road flood the joining well. Nimm chose a third bad option.

Crews chained the valve housing to two overhead beams. Jes reversed a small hushglass collar around the inspection mouth. Nimm drove wedges under the grate. When the collar absorbed the next pressure pulse, they lifted.

Black water erupted waist-high. A stone fragment shot through the gap and cracked the opposite beam. Three hooked marks gleamed on its face beneath algae and copper stain.

Jes slammed the collar free. The valve dropped. One beam split but held.

Nimm wrapped the release shard in oilcloth and placed it inside a ballast-tool chest with two witnesses’ seals. They did not understand all it proved. Hessa’s named law and the rubbing could explain more later. For now, the fragment had weight, custody, and a route connecting Parliament sabotage to the Hush draw.

Above them, boarding bells sounded at all three service docks.

Maraud’s forces had used the ballast emergency to advance. Shielded launches entered the eastern channels while Kett crews worked below. At First Dock, defenders dropped timber spars instead of stones. At Second, merchants opened a surrender gate without council authority. Third Dock’s labor crew locked both factions outside until a Merathi ram split the maintenance door.

Nimm reached First Dock as Kett workers retreated along the crane rail. A torsion stone hit the water beside a crowded transfer punt. The impact overturned it. Eleven civilians died before nets reached them. Twenty-seven suffered injuries from the overturn, boarding crush, and falling crane tackle.

Dead 56. Injured 102.

Maraud Sel came ashore behind a shield wall and ordered her soldiers to hold position rather than pursue families onto Nursery Hull. Nimm saw her only at a distance: wet coat, bare sword, one hand raised. Kett bolts struck shields around her. She sent an officer under white cloth to demand the joining engines.

“We are taking them apart,” Nimm told the officer.

“Then cease and preserve the remaining city.”

“The remaining city has voted with its pins.”

“Your council surrendered Second Dock.”

“One merchant opened one gate.”

“War has narrower grammar.”

“So does a noose.”

Negotiation lasted long enough for Forge Hull to free its southern secondary pins. Nimm ended it before the officer could count that movement as bad faith.

Hush troops captured First, Second, and Third Service Docks by midday. They raised no Merathi crown flag, only the blue square of emergency authority. Dock machinery remained largely intact. That outcome followed Varo’s order and still deprived Kett of its industrial heart.

Seven hulls now moved north. Seven remained linked.

The final separation plan depended on the southern route. South House, Ropewalk, Granary, and Shrine needed room to swing before following the others. Hush warships began closing that water in the afternoon.

Talren brought nine escorts into a crescent outside the route. “We can hold until dawn,” he told Nimm.

“You have eight fit crews pretending to be nine ships.”

“Correct.”

“And your shoulder?”

“Also pretending.”

He did not promise victory. Nimm trusted him more for it.

Through the night, civilians moved across the remaining bridges. Tokens passed into receiving baskets, then back to the ledger ward. The original 4,200 had all entered evacuation assignment. Eighteen strike dead, twenty-seven bridge dead, and eleven dock dead left 4,144 survivors before the final break. One hundred two injured remained inside that count.

Nimm had the clerk read the arithmetic aloud at every hull meeting. Numbers spoken publicly resisted heroic improvement.

Day Twenty-Nine rose red behind Hush sails.

South House cleared first. Its captain flooded the port ballast chamber to turn inside its own length. Ropewalk followed under tow from a Saltjaw outrigger. Granary carried most remaining water and could not risk a bolt below the painted load line. Shrine held the casualty ledgers, the Crown Register rubbing, and the sealed chest containing the release shard.

Council Hull remained connected to Market and School at the center. Speaker Memb refused priority passage.

“If the elected chamber leaves first, people will call it rank,” he said.

“If it leaves last, people may call it wreckage,” Nimm replied.

“Then they can argue over an intact body.”

The southern Hush squadron advanced before Granary completed its turn. Talren’s escort crescent divided. Three ships drew stone fire west. Two cutters laid smoke from wet kelp. Mothwake and the remaining escorts held the channel mouth.

No fire pots crossed the water. Torsion stones did.

One struck Ropewalk’s aft ballast chamber. The hull listed toward Granary, catching a transfer bridge full of water carriers. Nimm reached the pin station as the bridge buckled.

Seven people died in the impact and water. Fifteen were injured. A rigger with both legs broken kept the emergency vent open until the list corrected.

Dead 63. Injured 117.

Nimm painted the totals on the yellow board aboard Shrine.

Final count from the 4,200 civilians assigned to evacuation: 63 dead, 4,137 survivors. Of those survivors, 117 carried recorded injuries. Seven hundred seventeen had already continued north on relief craft under receiving custody. The fourteen harbor hulls carried 3,420 civilians between them, the number Kett’s independent flotilla would provision from this day forward.

Every token had a destination or a death witness. None remained in an unknown column.

The 117 injured did not occupy one convenient hospital. Forty-three rode Nursery with the surgeons and clean-water reserve. Twenty-nine remained with families on Well. Nineteen lay aboard Shrine because their testimony concerned the strikes. The rest spread among six hulls according to stability, infection risk, and whether moving them again posed greater danger than imperfect care. Every receiving deck signed a list.

Food followed a separate arithmetic. Granary held enough dry stores for twelve days at reduced issue, but separation placed three kitchens on hulls without grain mills and two mills on hulls without sufficient freshwater. Nimm assigned hand querns across bridges before the last pins came out. Market surrendered spice lockers for medical storage. School’s desks became bunk frames. Shrine’s ceremonial rain barrels entered the drinking count after priests and atheists agreed thirst recognized no sacred reserve.

Deaths altered ballast too. Empty assigned spaces could not simply remain empty on the light outer decks. Crews shifted water, tools, and sailcloth into the missing weight positions while families watched. Nimm hated that grief acquired pounds and trim angles. A hull did not become respectful by ignoring physics.

Granary cleared the channel. Shrine followed with two Saltjaw tow lines. Council, Market, and School broke their last shared pin at noon.

The joining well opened to daylight.

For generations, Kett children had dropped carved boats through its grating and watched them vanish under three streets. Now sea rolled between those streets. Council Hull drifted east for one dangerous minute before a harbor cutter caught its tow. School raised a patched square sail. Market’s vendors cut their awnings into signal flags.

Fourteen hulls moved separately.

Maraud’s southern squadron closed behind the last escort. A Hush tender lowered its glass curtain across the route. Wind died over the water. Mothwake barely escaped before the calm hardened. The three captured service docks and their cranes receded inside a ring of Merathi ships.

Kett had survived. Walking Harbor no longer occupied one place.

Nimm stood on Shrine Hull beside the yellow casualty board. Olt approached with the guild punch in his hand. He had taken it from the old contract office before Forge separated.

“You wanted a public hearing,” he said.

“I wanted us alive enough to hold one.”

“Will you accuse every worker who filled an order?”

“No. I will accuse the guild that trained us not to ask. Workers can answer in their own names.”

Olt looked back at the service docks. “They have the ledgers.”

Nimm tapped the sealed evidence chest. “Not all of them.”

Inside lay the cracked marked fitting, copied purchase codes, and the release shard. Hessa held the rubbing on the next hull under separate custody. Evidence had survived because no single deck carried all of it.

Northward, 717 transferred survivors vanished among relief sails. Around Kett, nine escorts took stations beside fourteen wounded hulls. Southward, the Hush curtain sealed the shortest road to the reef waters.

Nimm ordered the joining bells removed from storage and hung one on every hull. The first uneven chorus crossed the flotilla as each captain answered from a different patch of sea.

Chapter 14 — A Crown Written in Salt

Sivren stitched the last living leaf to a dead branch while surgeons counted Kett’s wounded on the deck below.

The mangrove cutting from Sen Veyra had survived the root-link with three leaves. Siege salt had browned two. Its final green leaf trembled inside a windbreak made from a child’s broken desk. Sivren bound the stem against a splint of old Kett oak, tucked wet moss around the graft, and pressed resin into the join.

“Dead wood cannot feed it,” Serel said.

“Dead wood can hold it still.”

Below the open hatch, clerks read the casualty ledger by hull. Sixty-three dead. One hundred seventeen injured. No lowered voices softened those numbers. Shrine Hull carried nineteen of the injured, the records, and a ceremonial rain barrel that had become drinking water before the priests finished objecting.

Outside, fourteen pieces of Walking Harbor moved on separate swells. Their joining bells answered irregularly across the dark. Nine escorts held a loose ring around them. Southward, the Hush curtain showed as a blue-gray line where wind stopped. Three captured service docks stood beyond it under Merathi lamps.

Sivren tested the cutting’s root with one fingernail. Sap answered slowly.

Leth leaned over the evidence table. “If it dies, Sen Veyra loses our witness copy.”

“No. We lose a path to compare it.”

“Root-Mothers would call that the same thing.”

“They have called many possessions continuity.”

On the table waited three objects that had survived different kinds of removal. Hessa’s waxed-linen rubbing carried the Crown Register’s later border and three hidden hooks. Nimm’s oilcloth bundle held the release shard recovered from Kett’s ballast intake. Serel and Leth carried the Drowned Parliament cut pattern in their living witness lines, copied through the root-link before Eshara closed access.

No object belonged to Sivren. That made her the safest person available to align them.

Hessa entered with one hand against the cabin wall. She had rejected a chair twice, then accepted a stool after Garo pointed out that falling onto evidence would constitute a custody event. A clean cloth covered her left ear. Nimm followed with the sealed tool chest. Talren came last, his right arm still bound and his face gray from four days of command without proper sleep.

“Open under named witness,” Hessa said.

They named themselves. Nimm named Olt Barrow for the Kett guild faction, though he stood by the door and did not claim innocence. Jes Var represented the tender records.
