Leth and Serel spoke from opposed Veyran standings. Island pilot Ira carried the unnamed islanders’ limited permission: observe routes, reveal no island name before seawater. Council Speaker Memb represented Kett. A refugee delegate named Afa Mor stood for the 717 transferred north and the families still among the hulls.

Sivren added one empty place at the table.

“For conquered islands not present to appoint anyone,” she said.

Memb frowned. “An empty stool cannot witness.”

“No. It can remind witnesses of absent standing.”

Hessa recorded the distinction.

Nimm broke the tool-chest seals. The stone fragment inside measured from Sivren’s wrist to elbow and weighed more than its size suggested. Three hooked generation marks crossed one face. The reverse carried grooves from the Parliament manifold and a smear of Kett copper where it struck the ballast beam.

“Pressure moved it from the ruin to us,” Nimm said. “The Hush siege draw supplied the pull.”

Jes set purchase-code slates beside it. “The manifold fed a relay network. Kett’s intake intersected an old branch because your harbor dampers used the same standard diameter.”

Olt folded his arms. “A diameter does not prove intended connection.”

“No,” Sivren said. “Its route does.”

She covered the evidence table with a thin bed of salt. Water made salt visible through change, not color. She drew the root scar copied from Sen Veyra: a blank shaped like a branching passage west through the shell gyre. Next she laid the rubbing over it. The Crown Register border crossed the scar’s narrowest point. Finally, Nimm turned the shard until its three hooks aligned with the hidden marks beneath that border.

They fit imperfectly.

Olt seized on the gap. “Different scale.”

“Different records,” Hessa said. “A wall register and a portable clause stone.”

“Or three unrelated hooks common to old masonry.”

“A useful objection,” Sivren said.

Olt looked cheated by her agreement.

She rotated the shard twelve degrees. Its manifold grooves aligned with the pressure sequence Hessa had heard inside the captured storm. Sivren marked the sequence as intervals in the salt. The seven storm paths converged beyond the table’s edge. The shard’s old hooks pointed away from that convergence, toward the repeated gaps in the root scar.

“The marks carry two relations,” she said. “Crown alterations direct obligation inward toward a single register. Older endings direct it outward toward parties who must return.”

Hessa lowered her fingers beside the rubbing without touching. “At the Parliament, each obligation ended after a named generation unless living parties renewed it.”

Afa Mor asked, “Ended how? The debt disappears?”

“No.” Hessa took time before answering. “The command expires. Whether repair remains owed depends on the harm and living parties. We have not found the complete procedure.”

“Convenient for kingdoms that stole from people now dead.”

“It would be convenient if crowns had not removed it,” Hessa said. “They removed it because renewal allowed refusal.”

Sivren traced the later Merathi border. Its legal script declared listed coasts surety in perpetuity. A harbor obligation once renewed by people became attached to shore. A pilotage agreement became ownership of approach water. A warning duty became the right to station royal officers wherever danger might arise.

“Crowns did not merely lengthen promises,” she said. “They changed who could speak. A living party can refuse renewal. Territory cannot. Descendants born inside a claimed coast become evidence of consent because the register treats continued habitation as acceptance.”

Leth touched the root-scar drawing. “Sen Veyra did the same with tended water.”

Serel winced but did not contradict him.

The conquered island archive had used softer language than Merath: custodial approach, protective root claim, inherited tending. Its structure still converted an old invitation into territorial possession. Once the name entered the archive as property, every later islander appeared inside a relation their ancestors could not end.

Sivren wrote the conclusion in salt across the center of the table.

FINITE OBLIGATION ALTERED INTO HEREDITARY TERRITORIAL OWNERSHIP.

Hessa tested the sentence against three entries on the rubbing. A refuge clause for one storm season became permanent crown access to a harbor. A shared-pilot clause became the right to appoint pilots and collect their fees. A repair compact after a volcanic winter became levy authority over two islands whose current inhabitants had never witnessed the original words.

Each transformation followed the same cut. Remove the generation ending. Replace renewal with presumed continuation. Attach continuation to coast rather than named parties. Once completed, the legal machine could call resistance breach instead of refusal.

Afa pressed one finger into the salt beside the refuge entry. “My mother’s island paid harbor levy for forty years because three boats sheltered there during a storm before she was born.”

Memb answered, “Kett charged joining dues to families born after their grandparents requested a berth.”

“Could those families leave with their homes?”

“Before today, rarely.”

Fourteen separated hulls moved outside the cabin walls. Kett had just made possible what its own compacts often denied. The comparison unsettled Memb enough that he asked the clerk to append it to the record.

Olt read the formula aloud and objected to the word ownership. Guild contracts used custody, license, and maintenance right. Leth answered with the test that had emerged from Sen Veyra: if living occupants could not end the relation without surrendering place, a gentler noun changed no material fact.

No pressure answered. They were not over open seawater, and no one had spoken a promise. The sentence carried only the force people chose to give it. Every witness still had to decide what that force required.

Memb read it twice. “This could invalidate half the harbor compacts in Sarravai.”

“Not by itself,” Hessa said. “Evidence requires hearings. Parties require standing. Harms require repair.”

“While sixty-one warships move storms toward us?” Afa asked.

“Then speed and fairness are both material constraints.”

Nimm turned the cracked Barrow fitting beside the shard. “Hush uses the inherited network even while claiming to silence it. Their arrays follow crown channels because those channels reach everywhere. They need the old ownership map to decide what water to empty.”

Olt tapped the purchase slate. “They bought fittings, not our politics.”

“They bought our decision not to ask,” Nimm replied.

“Workers would have gone hungry if we refused every state contract.”

“Then put hunger in the hearing. Do not put innocence there.”

The argument sharpened. Guild workers defended wages and separated design from deployment. Refugees described calm corridors that stranded boats outside licensed routes. Serel defended nursery protection while admitting Eshara’s property strata had shaped the Hush index. Leth wanted every conquered name released immediately. Hessa warned that speaking old names over open water without knowing attached oath language could wake more storms.

Ira, the island pilot, listened until everyone else had spent certainty.

“You all speak of records,” she said. “My people survived by refusing one. If your correction creates a better record that must contain us, we will refuse that too.”

Sivren looked at the empty stool. “Then refusal must remain on the map.”

“A mark for refusal still marks our place.”

“Then perhaps the map carries the route around your absence and no reason for it.”

“Perhaps. You do not decide today.”

“No.”

That no mattered more than agreement. Sivren drew no line for the island corridor.

Hessa asked each witness to state what the evidence did not prove. The practice irritated those eager to publish and calmed those afraid of an invented certainty.

The shard did not prove every old oath carried an ending. The rubbing did not show the complete pre-crown procedure. Root scars did not reveal all parties harmed by Veyran claims. Purchase records did not show how hushglass itself was made. Pressure routes did not establish who should control them after Hush.

“What does it prove strongly enough to act upon?” Talren asked.

Until then he had remained near the hatch, counting bells and sail positions beyond the cabin. Command entered his questions through consequence rather than volume.

Sivren rearranged the records by route instead of date.

Kett-made fittings had traveled south under purchase codes bearing a double-reef stamp. Jes identified that stamp as final inspection for Merath’s submerged furnace yards. The Parliament manifold carried the same thread gauge and pressure numbering. The siege arrays had drawn the shard along that network. On Sivren’s stolen western charts, three blank scars continued south from Kett and ended under a volcanic reef omitted from ordinary trade maps.

Nimm leaned close. “Kett guild ledgers call the supplier Deep Ash Works.”

Jes shook her head. “Naval orders call it Station R-Seven.”

Hessa looked at the seven pressure lines. “A relay?”

“A manufacturing and repair node,” Jes said. “Every large glass frame passes through it before fleet installation. I never saw the furnaces. Tender clerks received sealed assemblies at the outer dock.”

Sivren extended broken lines through the salt. The scar route, purchase route, and deep-pressure route converged beneath the same reef.

“Reef forge,” Nimm said. “Hullwright slang. We joked about Merath firing glass underwater because no sane guild wanted the heat on land.”

Nobody laughed now.

If they seized the reef forge, they could interrupt array replacement and learn how the network attached to crown channels. If they destroyed it blindly, a pressure backlash might strike every connected coast. If they left it, Varo’s accelerated convergence would continue while Kett drifted without service docks.

Memb wanted to turn north and protect civilians. Afa said the 717 relief passengers would remain targets as long as Hush controlled safe routes. Olt proposed publishing the evidence and negotiating international inspection. Leth asked which international body had not inherited a crown compact. Serel argued Sen Veyra might supply root-ships if shown direct proof of archive misuse, though Eshara would demand custody.

Talren placed his left hand on the chart edge. “We cannot command fourteen civilian hulls into a campaign.”

“No one asked you to,” Nimm said.

“I am preventing myself from asking badly.”

He proposed escort choices. Kett hulls could move north with civilians, remain near the unnamed island corridor under consented bearings, or join a southern supply route outside direct attack. Each escort pair could accept or refuse. Mothwake would go south regardless, with volunteers and evidence copies rather than the sole originals.

Hessa considered the shard. “The forge connects manufacture, route control, and Parliament sabotage. It can answer questions the rubbing cannot.”

“It can also kill us,” Olt said.

“So can staying.”

Sivren watched the salt lines shift when Shrine Hull rolled. No route remained fixed even across one table. Evidence did not point like an arrow. It narrowed the choices responsible people could defend.

Memb called witnesses from each Kett hull by signal bell. The resulting council occupied decks, speaking cones, rope messages, and lamp codes across half a mile of water. Some hulls rejected any further war. Nursery offered surgeons but no fighters. Forge volunteered repair crews. Mudlark chose the northern relief route. Shrine accepted evidence custody. Council agreed to a campaign supply link only if civilian hulls retained withdrawal rights.

The three Saltjaw outriggers chose south independently. Ira and Oru would pilot Mothwake around their island’s absent route without naming it. Jes Var asked to join as a technical witness. Her former tender crew divided: seven volunteered, five chose relief craft.

No single vote created coalition authority. Decisions overlapped enough to make movement possible.

At sunset, Hessa spoke the resolution into three witness basins, each aboard a different hull. They would seize the reef forge to stop Hush production, preserve its records, and investigate the deep pressure network. No polity surrendered independent withdrawal. No evidence entered exclusive custody. Civilian protection remained an obligation subject to review by those carrying the risk.

The sea received no magical oath because the basins stayed covered from open water. After learning what permanent wording had done, nobody wished to create another binding instrument in haste.

Sivren still faced a practical danger. Linen could burn. Stone could sink. Living witness lines could be closed by Eshara. The evidence required a relational copy that could travel even if every object separated.

She uncovered her forearm.

Veyran bark tracery ran pale beneath copper-brown skin, branching from wrist to elbow. A body-root memory could hold route relation for weeks, sometimes months. It carried no authority beyond the body, but another living root could read it with consent. Making one required surrendering a detailed personal memory equal to the pattern’s complexity.

Serel saw her preparing the resin needle. “Use the cutting.”

“It may not survive tonight.”

“Use my witness line.”

“Eshara can close yours from Sen Veyra.”

Leth offered his arm. “Mine carries fewer archive locks.”

“Mine carries the crime that shaped the blank.”

Sivren chose the memory before courage could make the cost theatrical. Not her mother’s face. That image had already endured one altered return, and she would not spend it for effect while lesser memories remained. She chose her first complete solo chart: a tidal garden east of Sen Veyra, drawn at age twenty-three. She remembered every green channel, the pride of correcting her teacher, and the small red boat that had followed her home.

She named the memory to witnesses.

Then she pushed the resin needle beneath the first branch of tracery.

Pain spread like roots seeking water. Serel held the rubbing above Sivren’s arm without touching. Hessa read the generation marks. Nimm called the shard’s angles. Leth supplied the root-scar branches. Sivren did not copy words or claim ownership. She copied relations: later border over older hooks; hooks toward living parties; scar shaped by removal; pressure route south to the forge.

The map entered her skin as dark branching lines.

The tidal garden began to leave.

First the red boat lost its shape. Then the corrected channel emptied from her mind. Pride remained as a fact without scene. She knew she had completed a first solo chart and could no longer picture one line of it.

When the needle came free, her forearm carried a salt-white crown outline crossed by three dark roots. It looked like a crown written in salt and already being opened by water.

Serel tested the body-root copy with two fingers and withdrew before taking more than consent allowed. “Readable.”

“Authoritative?” Sivren asked.

“No.”

“Good.”

Outside, joining bells gave the departure sequence one hull at a time. Northbound relief craft changed sail. Southern volunteers transferred stores to Mothwake and three Saltjaw outriggers. Talren’s command leads gathered on separate decks rather than one quarterdeck.

Sivren wrapped her arm in damp cloth. The lost garden left no image when she searched for it. Ahead, the route south began as three disputed lines around a Hush-held channel.

On Day Twenty-Nine, with Kett moving apart behind them, the first southern sails turned toward the reef forge.

Part III

Chapter 15 — The Long Wake South

The first burial plank jammed against Mothwake's lee rail before dawn.

Talren put his left hand beneath it and lifted. His right shoulder answered with a bright internal scrape, the old burn drawing tight from collarbone to blade. He kept that arm against his ribs while two deckhands freed the warped end. Beneath the sailcloth lay a Kett riveter named Pask Barrow, no kin of Nimm's despite the harbor surname, forty years old and killed when Service Dock Three folded under Hush stones.

Fourteen harbor hulls moved south in a ragged double column. They no longer resembled Walking Harbor. Each carried a neighborhood cut away from streets, cisterns, markets, and the iron bridges that had made those separate decks a city. Nine escort ships worked the flanks. Between them traveled 3,420 civilians, the survivors remaining after Kett's sixty-three dead and the northward transfer of 717 others, with the injured laid under every awning that could take a hammock.

Talren had ordered the funerals staggered. A fleet under pursuit could not lower sail together, not even for grief.

Nimm stood across the plank with a brass tally plate tucked under one arm. “Pask signed the south coupling on Hull Nine. If the coupling fails, I intend to accuse him personally.”

“You'll need a court with a longer reach than Hessa's.”

“I'll build one.” Nimm touched two broad fingers to the cloth over Pask's brow. Their gold tooth did not show. “Ready.”

The crew tipped the plank. Sailcloth, stones, and body entered the long wake between Hulls Eight and Nine. No oath followed. Kett custom held that the dead owed no promise to the living, and Talren had come to distrust any funeral that enlisted a corpse. The harbor bells gave one low stroke from fourteen decks, passed southward so the ships never broke formation.

By sunrise they had buried six. Forty-seven waited in sailcloth, and ten of the siege dead had already gone down with the service docks. The remaining bodies lay in Hull Four's drained eel market, packed in salt intended for food. Every burial reduced the fleet's stores and increased the number of hands too grief-struck for a watch.

Talren climbed to Mothwake's quarterdeck with his right arm bound close by a loop of canvas. The carrack had lost eleven crew at Rainward. Nine more still carried severe wounds, though three could now take seated work at the pumps and sail lockers. Siege transfers had filled gaps with Kett volunteers, but numbers did not turn strangers into a watch. He had sixty-six of the Rainward crew fit, nine injured, and twenty-three new hands who knew harbor decks better than a pitching mast.

South ran beyond the bow, a dark blue road combed by following wind. North, smoke still smudged the low sky above Kett's captured service docks. Hush scouts appeared whenever the swell lifted: two narrow lateen ships keeping beyond torsion range, mirrors shuttered, counting damaged sails.

“Fresh-water report,” Talren called.

The route circle gathered around the binnacle, though none of them answered to that name yet. Sivren came with three strips of wet root-fiber wrapped over her forearm. Nimm brought the tally plate. Hessa descended from the foredeck slowly, one hand moving from stay to rail when the ship rolled. Orra Kesh arrived from a Saltjaw outrigger by rope chair and refused the offered arm on landing.

Nimm read without ceremony. “Eleven days at half measure if nobody washes, brews dye, boils unnecessary broth, or discovers a religious need for baths. Eight days if Hull Twelve's stern cistern keeps leaking.”

“It keeps leaking because the stern seam keeps opening,” said Talren.

“An admirably naval diagnosis.”

Sivren laid the root strips across the chart board. Their pale fibers curved toward separate southern bearings. “We can reach the reef shelf in three days if this wind holds. Four if it backs east. The captured docks gave Hush the inside current, so the direct line invites interception.”

Orra studied the strips without touching. “Your roots remember a coast my people never consented to map.”

“They remember water pressure and mineral taste.”

“They also remember names taken into your archive.”

Sivren's face tightened. “Yes.”

One word prevented the familiar exchange from becoming an argument. Talren marked that. A month earlier he would have seized the silence and issued a course. He could feel the order forming now, clean and useful: two points west until noon, then south by the least-bent root strip. Every ship would know whom to blame if it failed.

Instead he set four wooden cups on the chart. “We cannot carry one admiralty across fourteen civilian hulls, three Saltjaw outriggers, Kett escorts, and Mothwake. We tried one line of command at Nerra Shoals. Six refugee ships paid for how quickly I could make a decision.”

The deck moved beneath his boots. Pain crossed his right shoulder when he reached for a fifth cup, so he used his left.

“Four command circles,” he continued. “Route. Defense. Stores. Civilian passage. Each circle names one speaker for signals, but a speaker does not own the ships represented. Any ship may refuse an instruction. Refusal must be signaled, and the circle replans around it rather than treating it as mutiny.”

Hessa watched him with the stillness she used before finding a weak clause. “Can the defense circle order a civilian hull to hold position under attack?”

“No. Civilian passage holds that decision.”

“Can stores deny water to a ship that refuses a route?” Nimm asked.

“No punishment through supply.”

Orra tapped the western root strip. “If Saltjaw refuses a Veyran bearing?”

“Route offers another, or Saltjaw names its own and accepts the separation risk.”

“Then this arrangement can produce five fleets at the instant danger arrives.”

“It already contains five fleets,” Talren said. “The cups merely admit it.”

By noon the circles had no seals, no flags, and too many opinions to fit any captain's table. They also produced a course. Two Kett escorts would range west for water squalls. Saltjaw outriggers would screen the direct current because their shallow hulls could cross the reef teeth if pursued. Mothwake would keep near the hospital hull rather than lead. Sivren's route strip would guide those who accepted it; Orra's pilot offered a star bearing for ships that did not.

The first test arrived before the ink dried. Hull Twelve signaled that its stern cistern had split another handspan. Nimm demanded a half-day slowdown for a diver patch. The defense circle reported Hush scouts closing from three points. Civilian passage refused to abandon the leaking hull or transfer its seven hundred people in open swell.

Talren listened from the quarterdeck while the four signal speakers argued by flags and horn. His instinct supplied the answer immediately: turn the fleet west, put Mothwake between scouts and hulls, send every available diver. That answer depended on his view, his timing, his confidence that everyone else could execute what only he had considered.

He folded his arms, regretted what the motion did to his shoulder, and kept silent.

A Blue Keel trader named Foam Ledger had joined the evacuation without entering anyone's command. Its master offered two spare water bladders but refused to halt. Saltjaw proposed towing Hull Twelve while Kett divers worked beneath a drag cloth. Sivren found a shear current that would carry the scouts past if the fleet slowed by precisely one knot instead of stopping.

The plan emerged crooked, contested, and better than Talren's. Hull Twelve kept moving. No civilian transferred. The scouts overshot by late afternoon and spent three hours clawing back northward.

Near sunset, five Ironwake hulls rose behind them.

Their sails carried no Hush blue and no coalition signal. Black iron ribs armored their bows. The center vessel flew Keth Varu's split-hook pennant, high enough for every Kett child on the afterdecks to see. Panic traveled faster than orders. Harbor Hull Six loosed a torsion arm before its escort cut the trigger rope.

Keth's ships held beyond bolt range. A skiff crossed the intervening water under a white oar blade, carrying Keth and four rowers.

Talren received him on Mothwake's waist. Hessa stayed by the mainmast. She and Keth exchanged no greeting.

“You have made a council out of rigging,” Keth said after Talren explained the circles.

“Rigging holds because strain goes more than one way.”

“It also kills sailors who stand inside a fouled turn.” Keth looked toward the separated harbor hulls. “Varo's scouts counted you this morning. His southern patrol will count you tonight. You will not reach the forge by the charted entrance.”

Hessa's throat plates tightened, a subtle lifting along her neck. “Why tell us?”

“Because I want to see whether you damage it before Varo uses it to empty another coast.”

“That sounds like alliance.”

“It sounds like observation.” Keth's gaze settled on her left ear, where old blood had darkened the cord beneath her earring. “Do not draft me into your judgment, Wake-Judge. Ironwake has not accepted your evidence or your authority.”

Talren asked, “What lies off the charted entrance?”

“Three glass towers under the reef lip. They can flatten a current and leave a ship without steerage. The service tunnels draw cooling water from the south face. Goblin guild stamps mark the old valves.”

Nimm, standing near the fore hatch, went rigid.

Keth saw it. “Your people built more of this war than the fittings in Kett.”

“My guild sold work,” Nimm said. “You sold ships.”

“I know what I sold.”

That ended the exchange. Keth returned to his skiff without offering charts, pilots, or a promise. His five hulls took station half a league east, close enough to intervene and far enough to deny belonging.

On Day Thirty-two, the wind failed. Crews sweated at tow sweeps while the sea lay polished under a white sky. Repairs moved from emergency to exhaustion: reefing lines spliced from funeral cord, sail patches cut from market awnings, pump leather boiled soft and nailed over cracks. Talren spent two watches transferring injured people away from a hull whose center beam had begun to complain. He used his left hand for ladders and let a young deckhand buckle his sword belt when his right fingers lost strength.

No one pretended not to notice. That embarrassed him less than it once had.

At midwatch the hospital hull asked for lamp oil, clean linen, and one ship's carpenter. Stores could spare only two. The old admiral in Talren wanted to choose by military value: oil to surgeons, carpenter to the failing beam, linen postponed. Civilian passage sent back a different account. The linen request came from a fever deck where bandages had already been washed three times in brackish water. Nimm's stores circle offered sailmaker's cotton if defense released two bolts from the emergency patch locker. Defense refused until the hospital hull agreed to carry six spare fire pots farther from the children sleeping under its forecastle.

The exchange took nineteen signal hoists. A centralized order could have settled it in three.

Yet the settlement exposed needs Talren would never have seen from Mothwake. The fire pots moved to an escort. Cotton crossed by skiff. A retired bridgewright from Hull Two volunteered for the beam and brought two apprentices, leaving the naval carpenter free to repair a steering yoke. Oil reached the surgeons before sunset because a Saltjaw cook surrendered a ceremonial jar without being ordered to do so. No circle obtained everything. Nobody had to pretend that the missing thing carried no cost.

Talren entered each transfer in the command log with the name of the group that had consented and the group that had refused. If the fleet survived, memory would not polish their arguments into effortless unity.

The command circles changed with each watch. A Kett cooper spoke for stores at noon; an island pilot held route by dusk. Hessa joined civilian passage and forced the escorts to reserve two skiffs for rescue rather than boarding. Orra moved between Saltjaw decks, arguing that refusal carried a duty to warn others early enough to survive it. By night, the fleet could change bearing through four flag exchanges without waiting for Mothwake.

Day Thirty-three opened under falling ash.

At first Talren mistook it for rain against the dark sea. A flake landed on his cuff, gray on one side and faintly blue on the other. More settled across the deck. South of them, the horizon pulsed from beneath, an underwater glow revealing the black comb of a reef.

Then the ash plume rose. It did not climb from an island. It came through blowholes in the reef shelf, six columns breathing at different intervals, each exhalation carrying the mineral reek of hot salt and quenched glass.

Sivren's three route strips curled away from it. Keth's five ships shortened sail to the east. Across the flotilla, command-circle flags began to rise before Talren reached for a signal line.

Nimm came beside him with a guild key held in one broad hand. “Those cooling valves use Kett teeth.”

Below the southern horizon, the forge breathed again. This time every ship in the long wake turned toward it by its own helm.

Chapter 16 — The Forge Beneath the Reef

Nimm's diving bell struck the service grating hard enough to shear one of its three guide ropes.

The bronze shell spun in green darkness. Four hullwrights crashed shoulder to shoulder inside it while the air pipe whipped against the roof. Nimm caught the valve wheel with both hands, planted their boots against a riveted seam, and counted the impacts traveling through the metal.

One against stone. One against stone again. Then a hollow knock beneath them.

“Grating,” they said. “Not rock. Barrow luck continues its disappointing run.”

Daro Venn, the youngest diver, had bitten his lip. Blood floated in the bell's trapped air as red beads. “We lost the west guide.”

“Then we still possess an extravagant two.”

Nimm opened the voice tube. “Surface, slack east by one arm. Haul north by half. If you pull us into the intake wheel, I shall return as an invoice.”

The reply came warped through copper and water. Three knocks acknowledged. Above them, a Kett winch crew adjusted the lines from a low repair barge hidden in the reef's ash shadow. The bell scraped sideways. Blue furnace light seeped between bars below, and heat rose through twenty fathoms of seawater with the pulse of a sleeping animal.

Keth's warning had put them at the south face. Nimm's guild key had brought them through two old locks. The third rejected it.

They crouched over the lower hatch and fitted the brass key into a square socket exposed between the grating bars. It entered cleanly, turned an eighth, and stopped. A guildmaster had added a wartime tooth after the original installation. Nimm knew the habit. They had approved it on Kett contracts, a small alteration that kept buyers dependent on guild service.

“This valve contains a commercial improvement,” they said.

Daro stared at them. “Can you improve it back?”

Nimm took a narrow cold chisel from their belt. “With malice.”

They worked by touch while the bell breathed warm air from the surface pump. Each hammer tap risked carrying through the intake pipes. Above, the reef forge vented another cloud through its blowholes. Water surged downward. Pale flakes spiraled past the window, too light for ash and too angular for salt.

A fragment caught in the bell's outer mesh.

Nimm reached through the flooded tool sleeve and pinched it between leathered fingers. The flake showed blue-white under furnace glow. One side carried tiny parallel ridges, too regular for mineral fracture. Coral grew that way, laying witness bands around a name repaired in public water.

They put the fragment in a stoppered vial without speaking their thought.

The modified tooth snapped under the chisel. Nimm turned the key. The service grating folded inward, releasing a hot upward current that seized the bell and dragged it into the reef.

For six breaths they saw only bubbles. Then the guide ropes tightened. The bell rose into an air chamber lined with ceramic bricks. Its roof sweated fresh water. A maintenance platform circled the intake pool, and twelve figures waited there with hooked poles, faces wrapped in gray cloth.

Daro drew his knife.

“Put that away,” Nimm said. “If they meant to kill us, they would have cut the air pipe.”

A short human woman unwound her face cloth. Soot had settled in every line around her eyes. “Nimm Barrow?”

“That depends on whether you collect guild debts.”

“I collect fingers from feed screws. Yara Pell, powder floor.” She showed a left hand missing its smallest finger. “Your message said you had proof the glass was taking names.”

“I said we had evidence of erased records. Proof is why I came through your excellent machinery.”

Yara glanced toward the vial. “Then keep your voice low until we reach a dead pipe. The hush wardens hear vibration through the bricks.”

The forge workers lowered ladders. Nimm's team climbed from the bell into heat that struck harder than sunlight. Everyone on the platform wore felt soles. Tools hung in leather loops rather than against metal. Even the lamps had cloth around their chains. Forced silence governed the plant before the glass ever touched water.

Yara led them into a service throat no wider than Nimm's shoulders. Cooling pipes crowded one wall, ceramic feed lines the other. Each pipe carried a Kett stamp: a barrow under three nails, the old mark of Nimm's own hullwright guild. They had seen those stamps in order books twelve years earlier, beside euphemisms such as saline civic works and navigational quenching.

At the first junction, Daro touched one mark. “Did you make this?”

“My guild made it.”

“That isn't what I asked.”

Nimm remembered signing the quality release for six pressure valves. They had never visited the buyer. The fee had funded a new stern lock for Kett's poorest hull.

“I inspected the valve teeth,” they said. “I did not ask what passed through them.”

Yara's expression remained unreadable. “Then ask now.”

They crossed beneath the first furnace gallery. Through floor slits Nimm saw molds moving on a chain bed, long troughs of glowing blue-gray glass. Workers used padded paddles to skim slag without striking the mold rims. Overseers occupied enclosed booths above, where copper listening horns converged on every floor.

No songs timed the labor. No shouted warning preceded a pour. Hand signals carried danger: fist for pressure, flat palm for halt, two fingers at the eyes for warden. Silence slowed the work and made every worker responsible for watching three others. The inefficiency told Nimm that silence itself mattered to the process.

At a dead steam pipe, Yara opened a bolted inspection door. Sound from the furnaces softened.

“Salt comes from Merathi pans,” she said. “Ash comes from the reef vents. Powder arrives sealed from the north beds. We were told it came from dead reef limestone.”

“Dead limestone does not grow in bands,” Nimm replied.

Yara removed a rag packet from inside her shirt. Within lay more pale flakes and a strip of production ledger written in grease pencil. The ledger named batches by coastal site. Three names had been scraped away, but angled impressions remained in the paper.

Daro held the strip near the lamp. “Tern Hook. Kesh Narrows. The third starts with I.”

“Ironwake,” Yara said. “A warden beat a loader for speaking it.”

Nimm laid their vial beside her packet. Powdered witness coral. The phrase came into their mind whole and obscene: coral grown around accepted repair, ground until no name remained legible, then fused into glass that consumed other memories. The Hush did not merely clear old commands. It fed repaired witness into an industry of forgetting.

“We need the feed room,” Nimm said.

Yara retied the packet. “Twenty wardens between here and there. Two listening booths. A flood gate that can drown the powder floor if anyone takes the records.”

“How many workers?”

“Three hundred fourteen on this shift. Eighty-two housed below the flood line. Families on the east shelf.”

“Then we do not attack the furnaces.”

Daro frowned. “The fleet expects us to take the forge.”

“The fleet expects many things. Some of them will have to experience disappointment.” Nimm took out a slate and drew the intake system from memory. “We stop the mold chains, isolate the listening booths, vent the furnace heat through the western blowholes, and lock the flood gate open. Workers decide whether the plant continues.”

Yara gave a small, humorless laugh. “You think showing them dust will begin a revolt?”

“No. Missing fingers, lung scars, drowned loaders, stolen pay, and locked housing may have begun it. Evidence can tell them what their labor has been made to erase.”

They separated at the slag corridor. Daro and two divers followed Yara toward the powder floor. Nimm took the fourth hullwright, Kes, down a maintenance ladder to the drive chamber. The forge's machinery grew more familiar with every level. Kett ratchet drums turned the mold chains. Kett float governors regulated the cooling pools. A guild that praised itself for keeping refugees afloat had sold the same craft to a factory designed to make coasts forget.

At the drive chamber, six goblin mechanics worked under a Merathi overseer. One mechanic looked up as Nimm slipped through the service hatch. Recognition widened his eyes.

“Barrow?” he whispered.

The overseer heard the breath. He reached for the alarm cord.

Kes threw a felt tool bag over his head. Nimm crossed the gap, caught his wrist, and pushed him against the governor housing. Their four-fingered hand closed over his knuckles until the cord fell.

“No heroic blow,” Nimm murmured. “He knows the shutdown order.”

The oldest mechanic pulled the bag away just far enough for the overseer to breathe. “He knows how to drown us too.”

Nimm looked at the man's pale face. “Then he will spend the next hour discovering loyalty to workplace safety.”

They bound him with mold-chain packing. The mechanics needed little persuasion once Yara's ledger strip passed from hand to hand. Two had lost family names from reefs near occupied islands. Another had watched wardens empty three sacks into a furnace after ordering the crew to cover their ears. They showed Nimm how the emergency stops had been disabled with welded collars.

Removing collars under load required more than courage. It required jacks, wedges, oil, and agreement about which line could fail without throwing a mold through fifty bodies. Nimm assigned each mechanic a governor. Kes climbed into the west gear pit with a pry bar. Signals traveled by fingertip against pipe: three taps ready, two taps hold, one long scrape for danger.

The first mold chain stopped at midmorning on Day Thirty-five.

Above, furnace crews continued pouring for nine breaths before they understood. Molten glass filled stationary troughs. Workers pulled quench levers. Steam struck the galleries. Wardens shouted, breaking their own law of silence, and their listening horns filled with the roar.

Yara opened the powder-room doors.

Wind from the emergency vents lifted a white-blue cloud across the floor. For an instant Nimm saw ridges, branching cups, and tiny calcified hollows within it. Ground names covered the workers' sleeves.

Daro carried the release-shard rubbing copied onto black cloth. He held it beside the ledger impressions while Yara shouted the batch names over the steam.

“Tern Hook. Kesh Narrows. Ironwake Sound. They told us dead reef. They brought us witness beds.”

A warden ordered the loaders back to station. None moved.

The powder foreman, a broad Merathi man with burn patches over both cheeks, took off his silence mask. “My sister's repair grew at Tern Hook.”

Yara put the ledger in his hand.

He read the impressions twice. Then he crossed to the flood control and drove his iron lunch pin through the closing gear.

Revolt did not spread as one cheer. It moved through practical refusals. Furnace Team Four dropped its paddles. Ash carriers overturned carts in the warden passage. Mechanics locked the listening booths from outside. Two crews kept the cooling pumps running because killing the water would crack the reef and boil everyone in the lower galleries. Workers who wanted no part in fighting guided families toward the south intake instead.

Some sided with the wardens. A shift captain tried to restart the east chain and struck Yara with a hook. Kes broke his knee with a gear wedge. At the upper stairs, arrows came through steam. Daro took one beneath the collarbone. Nimm dragged him behind a salt bin while the floor foreman closed a ceramic baffle against the archers.

“Still extravagant,” Daro gasped when Nimm cut the shaft short. “Two guide ropes.”

“Save your breath for complaining to a surgeon.”

Outside, Talren's defense circle saw the western blowholes vent clear steam, the agreed signal. Saltjaw outriggers entered the reef teeth. Kett escorts landed teams at the family shelf. Mothwake stayed with the evacuation craft, honoring the plan even when Hush patrols appeared northward.

The family shelf became its own battle. Eighty-two workers had slept below the flood line, but nearly two hundred dependents occupied stone rooms beside the eastern vents. The forge company had kept them there as a guarantee against desertion. A single stair connected those rooms to the landing shelf, and the stair narrowed around a brine tank wide enough for only one adult at a time.

Nimm found the family stewards marking forearms with lampblack. One bar meant bound for the south intake. Two meant a relative remained on shift. A ring meant the person could handle a rope or carry a child. They had devised the marks before the revolt and would not abandon them for Kett's colored evacuation cords.

“Your boats count bodies,” said the senior steward, an elf named Vehra whose bark tracery had cracked from furnace heat. “We count households. If you split them, people turn back.”

“Then we count households,” Nimm said.

That choice reduced each skiff's rated load. It also prevented three panicked returns along the exposed shelf. Kett crews rigged belly lines between black rocks while Saltjaw pilots held outriggers against the suction of the cooling intakes. A Merathi grandmother refused a place until two Ironwake children from the next room boarded with her. Four powder loaders stayed to carry those whose lungs could no longer manage the stair.

When a Hush patrol rounded the north spur, the escorts asked permission to cut the belly lines and clear their firing arcs. Civilian passage refused. The lines forced one escort to take a bolt through its foresail rather than turn cleanly, but the last household reached open water intact. Nimm recorded the torn sail beside the names of the stewards who had made that cost necessary. A ledger that omitted refusal would turn rescue into ownership.

Nimm reached the control gallery with thirty forge workers and no illusion that they commanded them. Yara commanded the powder crews. The mechanics answered to the oldest among them, Tov Bar. Family stewards refused both and concentrated on getting children through the intake chamber. Their purposes overlapped for one hour.

The last warden booth stood above the main quench manifold. Its commander threatened to open every furnace draft and turn the galleries into a kiln. Tov showed Nimm a bypass no guild drawing included: a worker-cut pipe running through a washroom wall.

“Illegal improvement?” Nimm asked.

“Commercial,” Tov said.

They crawled through hot condensation and reached the manifold from below. Nimm drove their old guild key into the master socket. This lock still accepted it. One turn diverted seawater through six quench rings. Furnace light collapsed from blue to black in sequence. The commander opened his drafts onto dead fires.

By dusk the forge workers held every gallery.

Seventeen workers had died. Five coalition fighters had fallen in the landings. Thirty-one wardens lay dead, and eighty-six had surrendered. Nimm wrote the numbers before anyone could replace them with victory. Daro lived after a surgeon drew the arrowhead through his shoulder. Yara refused the coalition's offer of a governor's chair.

“We took our floor,” she said. “We did not take it for your war.”

“Good,” Nimm replied. “Chairs are poorly ventilated.”

During Day Thirty-six, workers and coalition examiners opened the records together. The ledgers traced shipments from witness reefs seized after accepted reparations. Guild contracts from Kett covered valves, governors, molds, and pressure locks. Nimm signed their name beneath a public statement of complicity, then listed the workers who disputed its wording. Agreement did not require one account.

Yara insisted that the first copy go to the labor floors rather than Hessa's hearing table. Tov demanded a second in Kett's guild hall, posted beside the contracts that had paid for the valves. Vehra asked for household testimony to travel with both, because production totals alone would erase the families used to keep workers from leaving. Nimm accepted all three demands. They spent the afternoon cutting duplicate letters into thin ceramic slips, material that furnace heat could not easily destroy and seawater would not blur.

The surviving coral sacks went into wet clay vaults under worker guard. No one proposed returning them to a furnace. Nobody yet knew whether ground coral could recover anything that had been taken from it.

At sunset Nimm crossed by skiff to Harbor Hull One. Deep in its center keel, below the water cistern and above the ballast stones, Kett had built a hull vault for civic deeds that had to survive separation. Three keyholders waited: Nimm for the hullwrights, a market elder for the civilians, and Hessa for the expedition hearing.

Sivren brought the Crown Register rubbing in an oilskin tube. Hessa carried the release shard wrapped in netted wool. Neither object resembled its consequence. One held copied cuts from an altered law. The other could fit beneath a child's palm.

Nimm inspected the vault seam, replaced a heat-warped gasket, and set both artifacts inside separate saltwood cradles. They added Yara's copied ledger strip and the vial of powdered witness coral as evidence, not as an answer.

Each keyholder turned a lock. The door sank flush with the keel wall.

“Hull vault sealed,” Nimm said. “Three keys, three custodians, no removal without all present or a recorded emergency refusal.”

Above them, fourteen harbor hulls adjusted lines for the next southward leg. Beneath the reef, furnace fires remained dark while forge workers posted their own watches. For the first time in twelve years, the guild key in Nimm's pocket opened nothing they wished to defend.

Chapter 17 — Names of the Betrayed

The bowl slid three fingerwidths across the hearing table before Hessa caught it.

Water lapped over the black rim. A paste of salvaged coral dust clung to the bottom, pale as ground bone beneath the awning. Thirty witnesses watched from the surrounding decks, and hundreds more stood along the rails of three Kett hulls lashed temporarily into a crescent. Any of them might have mistaken the movement for wind.

Keth Varu did not. His eyes dropped to Hessa's gripping hand, then to the width of her stance.

“Is the table moving,” he asked, “or are you?”

Hessa set the bowl back inside its salt ring. Her left ear carried a slow pulse of pain. “The record will show that Admiral Varu has challenged the judge's physical capacity before challenging her jurisdiction.”

“I challenge both.”

Yara Pell waited on the evidence deck with two forge workers. Nimm stood beside the sealed ledger copies, their broad hands empty and visible. Orra Kesh had brought nine Blue Keel tide-priests, though she had declined a witness bench and sat cross-legged on a coil of hawser. Saltjaw judges occupied the western rail. Ironwake captains held the eastern. None had agreed that Hessa possessed authority over the others.

That disagreement belonged in the hearing, not outside it.

“Then we begin with standing,” Hessa said.

She lifted a clay tablet. Its text had been drafted in four hands and accepted by none without amendment. “This proceeding does not bind Ironwake, Blue Keel, Saltjaw, Kett, the forge workers, or any absent polity. It preserves offered evidence, permits challenge, and records refusals. No finding creates command. Does anyone claim I have misstated their position?”

Keth raised one finger. “You call it a proceeding. I call it your attempt to turn my dead into a Saltjaw case.”

“Recorded.”

Yara spoke from the evidence deck. “Powder crews call it an accounting.”

“Recorded.”

Orra tilted her head. Shell beads clicked against the ceramic bands on her tusks. “The dead may call it something else, if you make them carry your grammar.”

“The sounds in oathwake are pressure-memory,” Hessa said. “Not speech by the dead.”

“Your court believes that distinction saves you from priesthood.”

“It saves the dead from being conscripted into our arguments.”

A murmur moved along the Blue Keel rail. Orra smiled without warmth. “Record that.”

Hessa did.

The hearing opened on Day Thirty-seven, one dawn after the forge seizure. Nimm established the chain of custody: dust gathered from a sealed feed room; three workers present; sack marks copied before removal; sample divided into four ceramic bowls; two held by workers, one by Kett, one offered for public testing. Yara named every hand that had touched it. Tov Bar described the furnaces. Vehra supplied the household testimony and refused to let the deaths disappear behind production totals.

Then Nimm read the ledger impressions.

“Tern Hook. Kesh Narrows. Ironwake Sound.”

Keth's captains did not move. Their stillness tightened the deck.

Hessa asked, “What do Ironwake records say stood at the Sound?”

Keth answered before his archivist could. “A naval yard. Abandoned during the ash famine.”

“Population?”

“Military.”

“Names?”

“No complete roll survives.”

Orra made a low sound in her throat. Keth turned on her.

“Do not pray at me.”

“I cleared my throat.”

Hessa looked down at the bowl. The dust held no voices she could hear while dry. Yet the first drop of open seawater had caused a pressure change against her throat plates, fine as a fingernail drawn over a drumskin. The sensation gathered whenever someone spoke Ironwake Sound.

She stated the risk into the record. “Wake-hearing this sample may injure me and may produce fragmentary impressions. Such impressions do not prove the dead have spoken. They must be tested against physical and living evidence. Any witness may stop the test.”

Keth's archivist, an elderly orc named Sarr Vetu, lifted her palm. “Ironwake has not consented.”

Yara raised hers in reply. “The powder workers have. We inhaled that dust for six years.”

“You do not own our names.”

“Nor do you own what happened to our bodies.”

The hearing stopped there for an hour.

Hessa did not mediate the dispute toward harmony. She asked who had standing in the material, where their harms differed, and which acts could proceed without forcing one party's claim over another. The workers allowed testing of their bowl. Ironwake refused to authorize interpretation of any surfaced name as an official ancestral roll. Keth demanded an Ironwake witness at Hessa's shoulder and the right to halt the water.

By noon they had a narrow consent.

The terms took another forty lines. No recovered syllable could enter the public roll until two non-magical sources supported it. Hessa would describe pressure, not intention. Sarr could interrupt after any name without giving a reason. Yara retained the sample and could remove it if Ironwake witnesses treated worker exposure as secondary evidence. Nimm built a spill tray from sail battens so no grain could wash through the scuppers into open water.

Keth demanded that every listener repeat the distinction between recognition and proof. Hessa made him state it first. He did, jaw set, then required each Ironwake captain to follow. Forge workers answered in their own wording. The resulting chorus lacked ceremonial beauty, but beauty had concealed too many seizures of standing.

Talren offered marines for the salt ring. Both sides refused. They chose four unarmed custodians instead: an Ironwake sailmaker, a forge loader, a Kett cistern keeper, and one of Orra's youngest priests. Each held a corner of the spill cloth. If any corner dropped, testing stopped. Hessa approved the arrangement because nobody could continue alone.

Nimm set the worker-held bowl inside the salt ring. Yara poured open seawater to the first mark. Sarr Vetu stood on Hessa's left. Keth remained outside the ring, refusing the appearance of participation while refusing equally to leave.

The dust drank water.

Pressure struck Hessa beneath the jaw. She gripped the table. White-blue grains lifted and turned through the bowl, each one dragging a tiny wake. Not voices. The patterns carried shapes of spoken names without breath or intention, remnants impressed when repair coral had grown around a public acceptance.

A syllable pressed against her throat plates.

“Har,” Hessa said.

Sarr's hand closed on the table edge.

Another pressure crossed the first at an angle, then broke. Hessa tasted copper. “Vetu. No. Vet… ara.”

Keth stepped forward.

The bowl flared cold. Water rose in a narrow ridge and tapped twice against the eastern rim. Within the contact came a crowd pattern: many names spoken in sequence, each followed by the same accepted phrase. Restitution for harbor dead. Payment accepted for those killed at the harbor. The legal form had grown the coral. Someone had harvested the reef after that repair.

Hessa followed the strongest line.

“Har Vetara,” she said. “Mek Orun. Talla Kesh. I have a child-name beginning Iru. I cannot hold the ending.”

A woman behind Keth sobbed once.

Sarr Vetu said, “Har Vetara served as yardmaster at Ironwake Sound. Mek Orun kept the west crane. Talla Kesh appears in my grandmother's famine book.”

“Military?” Hessa asked.

Sarr stared at Keth rather than answering.

The pressure deepened. Images did not come, but spatial burdens did: a line of bodies weighted at the ankles; a breakwater gate shut against people swimming from shore; names recited over repair water years later by survivors. Hessa's inner ear gave a wet click. The deck tilted toward the bowl.

She held the table until her knuckles blanched.

“Two hundred or more,” she said. “Families among them. The repair phrase identifies harbor dead, not enemy sailors. Someone accepted restitution. The coral grew. Later the reef entered Hush production.”

Keth crossed the salt ring and seized the bowl.

Water slapped onto the table. The pressure vanished so fast Hessa lost the direction of down. Her knee struck planking. Sarr caught her shoulder before her head reached the rim.

Around them, weapons came half free. Ironwake hands reached for Keth. Saltjaw guards reached for Hessa. Yara raised the clay evidence seal as if she might strike an admiral with it.

“Stop,” Hessa said from the deck.

Her own voice seemed to come through deep water. She waited for the boards to settle, but they rolled beneath her though the hulls lay moored. Blood reached her mouth from the left ear channel.

Keth held the bowl against his chest. “You will not perform them again.”

“They are not mine to perform.”

“Yet you made a court of them.”

“You required a witness at my shoulder. Your witness corroborated three names.”

Sarr helped Hessa sit. “I corroborated names. I did not accept your number or your account of the gate.”

“Then the record will separate them.”

Keth looked toward the Ironwake captains. Several would not meet him. The woman who had sobbed came through their line. She wore a shipwright's iron thumb ring and carried no rank cord.

“My house kept the child-name Iru Venn,” she said. “We were told fever killed her during evacuation.”

Keth's face changed by one degree. Hessa saw the change because she had spent half her life watching people decide which fact they could afford to recognize.

“Bring the famine books,” he ordered.

No finding closed the first day. Hessa could not stand without two supports, so she adjourned the hearing and refused Talren's offer to carry her. She crossed to Mothwake in a rope chair, jaw clenched as the horizon revolved. By night she could walk six steps along a cabin wall. On the seventh, her balance failed toward the left.

The evidence continued without her.

During Day Thirty-eight, forge ledgers were compared against Sarr's famine books, Saltjaw port records, and private household rolls brought from Ironwake hulls. Each source disagreed. The naval account listed ninety-one yard personnel. Household records named two hundred thirty-seven residents. A Merathi indemnity tablet recovered from the forge archive referred to pacification of dependent persons and compensation accepted by surviving hull authorities.

Living witnesses complicated every figure. A netmaker named Vaska Orun had been six when boats carried her from the north mudflats. She remembered soldiers closing the breakwater gate, but she placed the attack in late summer while the famine book placed it after first ashfall. Another survivor insisted the west crane had burned before the gate closed. Sarr's naval list marked that crane repaired three weeks later. None of the conflicts canceled the others. Together they showed panic unfolding across more than one tide, followed by an official record compressed into a single orderly evacuation.

Hessa asked Vaska who had spoken the restitution phrase over the later reef. The old netmaker pressed both palms to the table.

“Those who still had boats,” she said. “The shore families stood in the water, but hull authorities named the payment. My mother accepted grain because we were hungry. The record turned that grain into consent for everyone.”

Yara asked whether Vaska wanted the coral dust returned to Ironwake custody. Vaska looked toward Keth, then toward the powder workers whose lungs carried the material.

“Divide it,” she said. “No. That repeats what the mill did. Keep the sample whole and make the custody crowded.”

Her answer produced the three-seal arrangement Hessa later read into the record. It also angered captains who considered crowding a weakness. Keth did not silence them. He made each one state what authority they claimed, and several discovered that family descent, naval office, and possession of a surviving list did not amount to the same right.

No surviving source named the authority who had accepted.

Hessa listened from a bolted chair while witnesses argued across the awning. The phrase Ironwake massacre entered the provisional record at Yara's request and over Keth's objection. He accepted massacre only after Sarr read the indemnity language aloud. He rejected the implication that one event explained Ironwake politics or entitled any outside judge to direct its answer.

At dusk Keth brought Hessa a Hush dispatch.

Varo's seal had been cut away, but Maraud Sel's countersign remained. The order directed forge officers to increase Ironwake feedstock because reefs associated with disputed casualty claims offered low diplomatic cost. Batch reduction would weaken western calm-water deployment.

Keth placed the dispatch on Hessa's lap. “He knew.”

“His command knew.”

“Do not give him distance through grammar.”

“You defended Hush because it ended punishments imposed by dead crowns,” Hessa said. “That reason remains true even if this crime also remains true.”

His tusk bands flashed dark iron as he turned his head. “You expect me to thank you for preserving my consistency?”

“I expect you to choose without pretending the choice is simple.”

Keth walked to the cabin door. “I have recalled my liaison from Varo's fleet. The five hulls outside your line no longer report your movements to him.”

“That is not the same as joining us.”

“No.” He looked back. “If you call it joining, I will leave.”

“What do you call it?”

“Breaking a contract the other party broke first.”

He left the dispatch and took no Saltjaw token.

By Day Thirty-nine, Hessa could stand if she fixed her eyes on one vertical line and kept both feet wide apart. That method failed on deck, where every line moved. She attended the final session with a hand on the awning post. The crowd had doubled. Ironwake families occupied the front rail. Forge workers kept their own evidence table. Keth stood among his captains rather than beside Hessa.

Before the session, Sarr brought thirteen signal tablets removed from Keth's liaison boat. They carried Varo's requests for positions, water states, and probable coalition routes. Keth had answered the first nine. The tenth bore only a refusal mark. The last three remained blank.

Hessa asked whether the tablets belonged in the evidence chain.

“They belong in mine,” Keth said. “I supported Hush openly. I will not let your court rewrite that support as infiltration or confusion. We wanted calm water. We knew Merath would profit from controlling it. We judged that price smaller than crownstorms striking our grandchildren.”

“And now?”

“Now the price includes feeding our repaired dead into glass. I refuse it.” He put his seal across the unanswered tablets. “That statement grants you no command over what Ironwake does next.”

Hessa ordered his words copied without amendment. A cleaner story would have turned him from deceived enemy into grateful ally. The record instead kept the harder fact: Keth had understood much of Varo's bargain, accepted it for reasons that remained defensible, and broken only when its hidden cost crossed a line Ironwake itself would name.

She issued no verdict.

Instead she read the agreements and refusals: three names corroborated across pressure pattern and household record; fourteen more partially recovered but unverified; at least two hundred thirty-seven residents at the Sound; evidence of a closed breakwater; accepted compensation of disputed authority; later harvesting of repair coral for Hush production. Ironwake reserved judgment over its official dead. Workers claimed standing in exposure and coerced labor. Saltjaw courts offered archives but no jurisdiction.

Behind the three evidence seals, scribes pressed wet clay copies for every ship that had contributed testimony. No captain could sink the sole record by leaving, and no court could revise all copies without exposing the difference. The arrangement created inconvenience on purpose, a form of custody better suited to people who disagreed.

“Does any party dispute that this record must travel?” Hessa asked.

Keth spoke first. “It travels under Ironwake seal beside the worker seal. Not under yours alone.”

Yara agreed after adding household testimony as a third seal.

Orra rose from her hawser coil. She had remained silent through the reading. In her hands rested a staff as tall as Hessa's collarbone, made from dark saltwood around a core of lead shot. Three bands divided its grip: blue ceramic, black iron, and plain copper.

“My faith says old oaths live among us,” Orra said. “Your law says pressure repeats wording without a soul. Today neither belief kept living people from trying to own the names.”

She crossed the deck and held out the staff.

Hessa did not take it at once. “A judge carrying a priest's object invites conclusions.”

“A cousin carrying a weighted staff invites fewer broken teeth when she falls.”

Laughter passed through the rails, brief and edged with exhaustion.

Orra lowered her voice. “Listen to my condition. I will oppose any act that scrapes the dead clean merely to quiet the water. I will also oppose using them forever as commands no living person may refuse. Whatever you do at the end, preserve names. Not their ownership. Not our preferred meaning. Their names.”

Hessa closed her hand around the grip. Weight settled through her arm to the deck. The lead core lagged a fraction behind each roll, giving her body a second measure of motion. For the first time in three days, she knew where upright lay without looking.

“I cannot promise the means,” she said.

“Good. You do not know them.” Orra released the staff. “Promise the demand remains in the hearing.”

Hessa faced the three evidence seals: Ironwake, worker, household. “Recorded.”

Keth signaled his five hulls to raise anchor. They moved west to call the scattered Ironwake captains, outside Saltjaw command and outside Varo's. Hessa remained on the joined decks with the staff planted beside the salt ring, listening as families began reading the recovered names in their own orders.

Chapter 18 — The Elf Who Burned Her Maps

The current closed around Mothwake while Sivren's sounding lead still descended.

One moment the carrack moved south at four knots under reduced canvas. The next, mangrove roots rose from blue water in three converging lines, their red tips knitting into a gate ahead of the bow. The lead line dropped straight beneath Sivren's hand. Sail stayed full. No wake left the stern.

“Back the forecourse,” Talren called.

Crew hauled with disciplined speed. His right arm remained strapped close to his body while his left directed the braces. Behind Mothwake, fourteen Kett hulls lost steerage one by one. Orc outriggers lowered sweeps before they could drift together. The current had not stopped. It circled beneath them, holding each vessel in a separate basin of rooted water.

A green shoot climbed the lead line.

Sivren let it coil around her wrist. Pale bark tracery brightened under her skin, answering the root-link. Leaves opened along the shoot despite the salt. Each carried a line of Eshara Othen's formal hand.

RETURN THE WESTERN ROOT CHARTS.

SUBMIT THE DROWNED-PARLIAMENT COPY FOR ARCHIVE REVIEW.

PASSAGE SOUTH REMAINS CLOSED UNTIL RESTITUTION.

Nimm leaned over the rail. “Your family sends unusually large letters.”

“Root-Mother Eshara has always preferred an audience.”

The roots tightened. A Kett escort yawed close enough to scrape Hull Seven. Sailors threw padded fenders between them. Farther north, Hush scout mirrors blinked from the ash haze. The gate had trapped the coalition in view of pursuit.

Talren approached the chart table without touching it. “How long can she hold us?”

“This current runs through Sen Veyra's oldest navigation beds. Days, if the root crews accept her order.”

“Will they?”

Sivren read the growth. Some root tips carried fresh cuts where sap workers had tried to prevent the closure. Other strands had been reinforced with archive grafts, thick pale wood taken from map chambers. Sen Veyra did not hold one intention. Its conflict had reached across half the sea and become a barrier under their keels.

“Long enough,” she said.

She sat on the deck and pressed both palms around the shoot.

The root-link took her through cold.

Mothwake vanished. Her awareness traveled along wet fibers past reef stone, buried anchor chains, and the mineral tastes that maps translated into route. Distance arrived as strain rather than miles. At the far end, Sen Veyra opened around her in broken glimpses: council platforms among mangrove crowns, sap-cutters clinging to ladders, archive pools dark beneath woven roofs. Hundreds of hands touched the linked roots. None of them shared one memory of what the gate should protect.

Eshara waited on the root-throne.

Its living arms had grown around her shoulders over seven decades. Copper leaves framed a face lined by age and stored decisions. Behind her, the archive basin carried thousands of map memories in layers of rootwood. Sivren could feel the western charts among them, both the versions she had stolen and the authoritative copies from which every sanctioned navigator had learned.

“You bring armed fleets into Veyran current,” Eshara said through the link. Her mouth did not move. Thought traveled as pressure in the roots. “You carry stolen archive knowledge in your body. You demand passage without review.”

“You closed water around three thousand displaced civilians.”

“I stopped warships.”

“The civilians float among them.”

“That arrangement belongs to the commanders who placed a city in a battle column.”

Sivren sent back the image of fourteen separate hulls, laundry strung beneath patched awnings, fever cots lashed clear of spray. She did not frame it as accusation. Roots carried images poorly when sharpened by rhetoric.

Eshara flinched, but the gate did not loosen.

Voices entered the link from side branches. Western sap-cutters accused Eshara of using their current beds without consent. Archive stewards demanded Sivren return the charts before Merathi seizure made recovery impossible. Three descendants of conquered islanders asked why Sen Veyra still called those charts Veyran property when many routes had been taken from their pilots. Root-ship captains wanted the gate opened before Hush scouts brought fire to the mangroves.

One island claimant named Seln Aru forced an old pilot's cord into the link. Its knots described Osha Reef through swells, bird turns, and the taste of rain caught before landfall. The archive version translated those measures into fixed bearings and attributed them to a Veyran survey made nineteen years after annexation. Eshara argued that the archive had preserved a route Merathi occupation might otherwise have destroyed.

“Preserved under whose name?” Seln asked.

An archive steward answered that provenance could be corrected without burning navigational knowledge. A sap-cutter demanded to know why correction had remained pending for three generations. Root-ship captains objected that crews currently at sea could not navigate a debate about attribution. Each position carried a material risk. Delay exposed the fleet. Sudden deletion could strand traders. Continued authority renewed a theft each time a sanctioned chart displaced a local one.

Sivren felt the appeal of procedure with painful clarity. Review could separate useful depths from property claims, restore pilot names, and preserve safe routes. It could also last until everyone who remembered the seizure had rooted their recollections into an archive governed by the people reviewing them.

No speaker represented all elves. Sivren had never felt her culture more strongly than in the disagreement.

She drew the stolen maps forward inside her memory. Nine years ago, she had hidden a route correction to protect Sen Veyra's claim over the western shoals. Merath used the wrongness to seize three islands when their defenders grounded on a bar that Sivren knew had shifted. Later she stole the western charts to learn who had altered her copied memory. Every corrective act had preserved her authority over the map.

Eshara touched the archive basin. “Return them, Sivren. Submit the Parliament rubbing. I will open a reviewed lane for your expedition and place Veyran pilots aboard every ship.”

“Pilots answerable to whom?”

“The root council.”

“Which root council? The throne, the sap courts, the island seats you still classify as dependent?”

“You mistake disorder for justice.”

“And you mistake one surviving archive for the coast.”

A sharp pull broke Sivren from the link. She opened her eyes on Mothwake's deck with her palms bleeding where bark had split the skin. The sun had moved a handspan. Hush mirrors continued blinking north.

Talren had not asked for an answer while she rooted. Four command-circle speakers waited near the mast. Civilian passage refused any attempt to ram the gate. Defense proposed sending outriggers through root gaps to harry the scouts. Route held three incompatible charts and no current.

Sivren unrolled her physical western copies.

The sheets were not paper. Thin layers of living bark had been pressed with root fibers that changed color under seawater. Every sanctioned bearing converged on a single line selected by archive authority. Notes in Sivren's hand corrected seasonal drift, but the structure still declared one route true and all deviations errors.

“What happens if you return them?” Hessa asked.

“Eshara opens one reviewed lane and places Veyran pilots over it.”

Her weighted staff rested against the rail. “A workable price.”

“Yes.”

“You dislike that I said so.”

“I dislike that you are right.”

Hessa looked toward the circling roots. “A hearing can accept necessary custody without accepting ownership.”

“A route followed by four thousand people becomes authority whether we call it temporary or not.”

“Then what can replace it before the scouts arrive?”

Sivren studied the trapped vessels. Each occupied a slightly different current basin. Every hull felt separate pressure. The roots holding them had made twenty-eight local measurements more precise than any central chart.

“Reports,” she said.

She asked route circle to distribute blank slates. A lead went over from every combat ship and harbor hull. Sailors recorded depth, pull, root angle, wind, and whether they consented to share the observation beyond their own crew. Saltjaw pilots used knotted cords rather than Veyran marks. Kett crews measured drift against floating wood chips. Ironwake hulls under Keth's distant signal sent only depth and refused location names. Free-island pilots supplied bearings relative to stars no crown chart recognized.

By sunset Sivren had a field of disagreements.

She did not average them. An average would have invented water no ship had measured. Instead she pinned each report to its own hull, time, draft, and method. The Kett chip drifts showed surface movement that meant little to a deep carrack. Ironwake leads carried reliable depth but withheld named position. Saltjaw cords captured the pulse between reef teeth while refusing archive notation. Sivren marked every limit beside the observation.

Children from Hull Seven ferried slate copies along the trapped line because signal flags could not carry enough detail. A Veyran mate on Root-of-Morning translated sap-cutters' breath intervals into bell strokes without claiming the two systems meant precisely the same thing. Talren's route circle rejected one eastern observation when the reporting ship admitted its lead had snagged on a moving root. Nimm corrected three hull drafts after accounting for water redistributed during the forge attack.

The work consumed hours the pursuit kept shortening. Hush scouts launched two sounding skiffs into the outer basins. Saltjaw outriggers drove them back with sling stones, then withdrew before the exchange became a fleet engagement. Every report arriving at Sivren's table had already cost rope, attention, or blood. That cost prevented her from treating the slates as neutral facts waiting for a superior mind.

No single observation opened a road. Layered together, they showed three places where the current gate leaked: a shallow western braid suitable for outriggers and Kett hulls at high water; a deep eastern pull that heavy warships could enter under tow; a narrow center pulse that opened whenever the archive grafts drew breath from the sea.

Plural bearings. The phrase felt clumsy and honest. A route could arise from several partial charts without declaring one of them master.

Talren studied the slates. “Can we move the fleet through?”

“Not while Eshara's authoritative maps keep correcting the roots toward their sanctioned line. The gate will close every local deviation as an error.”

Nimm touched the edge of Sivren's bark sheet. “Can you make the maps stop correcting?”

“Yes.”

The answer cost her before she acted. She felt the archive copies inside the link, rooted not only in Sen Veyra but in her own bark tracery from years of mapmaking. To burn an authoritative map meant removing its certainty from living wood. Root memory never surrendered detail freely. It required another detail of equal depth, a personal image dense enough to carry the fire through every copied line.

Sivren knew which memory lay nearest the western charts.

Her mother, Othei, had taught her to read current by watching mangrove pollen turn around a child's ankle. Sivren retained the facts: Othei's impatience with clean fingernails, her habit of saving sour fruit for the end of a meal, the low song she used while grafting roots. One visual memory remained untouched by prior map costs. Her mother's face at the western nursery, lit green through new leaves, eyes narrowed against rain.

She rolled the bark charts and tied them with root-fiber.

Hessa understood from her hands. “There may be another price.”

“There is always another price. The archive chooses which ones appear legitimate.”

“Do you want witnesses?”

Sivren looked around the deck. Hessa could witness law. Nimm could witness material. Talren could witness command. None could consent for Sivren's memory.

“I want contradiction,” she said. “If I later claim this act made the map free, record that it also destroyed knowledge other people might have needed.”

They agreed.

Night brought the Hush scouts within two leagues. Blue calm spread ahead of their bows as small glass arrays consumed the outer current. Talren's defense circle sent Saltjaw outriggers north to delay them without engaging. Civilian crews drenched awnings against fire.

Sivren placed the rolled maps in a copper basin on Mothwake's forecastle. Seawater would not burn them. Ordinary flame would char only the physical copies while the root archive retained authority. She needed living fire carried through the link.

Nimm packed the basin with kiln coals from the captured forge. Hessa poured open seawater into the salt channel around it. Talren ordered every signal lamp hooded so no commander could mistake the act for a fleet instruction.

Sivren cut the bark tracery over her heart.

Sap welled pale against her copper-brown skin. She fed a root-fiber from the western charts into the cut. Cold seized her ribs. Sen Veyra opened through the link again, broader now because hundreds watched from its branch courts.

Eshara rose against the root-throne's holding arms. “Do not confuse destroying authority with sharing it.”

“I don't.”

“The original bearings will vanish from navigators who depend on them.”

“Yes.”

“Island claimants may lose evidence against Merath.”

“Yes.”

“You will carry that harm.”

“If I can remember it.”

Sivren touched a coal to the fiber.

Green flame ran backward into her skin.

The physical maps curled in the basin. Lines lifted from bark as sparks, each sanctioned bearing burning brighter than the spaces around it. Through the link, archive roots cracked. Navigators cried out as copied certainty loosened. Sivren held the western nursery before her mind: rain in young leaves, Othei turning, light across cheekbones, the exact bend of her mouth before she laughed.

Fire reached for detail.

Sivren tried to give it the map instead. Root law did not bargain. The western shoals vanished first from her inner sight, then returned as uncertain depths gathered from the fleet. Othei's eyes blurred. Sivren clung to their color and lost the shape of her nose. She reached for the whole face and found pieces detaching from one another, no longer assembling into a person.

The last map line burned.

Her mother's face disappeared.

Love remained. Facts remained. Sivren knew who had held her fevered at nine, who had argued against her first archive copying, who had died during a salt blight thirty-one years earlier. She could not see Othei. Memory offered a blank under green light.

Sivren screamed once. The link carried it to every root court.

On Mothwake, the copper basin split. Hessa caught Sivren before she struck the deck, bracing awkwardly with the weighted staff. Around the fleet, root barriers lost their common pattern. Gaps opened wherever local water pressed.

Eshara remained in the link.
