An Ironwake captain boarded a Merathi tender searching for erased-name records and found fifty burned glass workers below. He put the search under seal and carried the workers out first. Eshara denied a root cradle to a royal archive chest so three wounded Blue Keel paddlers could lie above the waterline. Her own tenders protested. She made them record the chest's loss rather than pretending it had no value.

On Hull Four, civilians converted the dismantled deck into splints and stretcher poles. Children who had carried route tabs now matched rescued crews to ships from names shouted across the water. One child entered a nameless sailor as unidentified, breathing, red cord on wrist. The description preserved enough truth for the next search.

Talren watched choices spread without becoming one virtue. Some ships guarded prisoners. Others chased salvage. Two captains left for home before the dead count settled. A Merathi hull refused coalition boarding and still took twelve drowning free-island sailors from the current. Nobody's flag made the act pure.

Rain washed soot from Mothwake's deck. Independent ships crossed among enemies and allies, choosing whom they could reach before another hull went under.

Chapter 27 — When the Sea Forgot Kings

The deposed harbor seal struck the water and did nothing.

A Merathi clerk tried again from the basalt quay. He spoke the old opening order before twelve named witnesses, used the correct royal cadence, and pressed the bronze seal until his knuckles whitened. The entrance current continued running across the harbor mouth instead of dividing for crown traffic.

Three grain ships waited outside. Two fishing boats waited inside. Nobody knew who should yield.

“Try the south chain,” called a former customs officer.

“The chain has no authority either,” said a fisher.

“It still has iron,” the officer replied.

That settled the immediate question. Dockworkers hauled the physical chain aside. Fishers backed into a cove. The grain ships entered one at a time under shouted depths from local pilots whose grandparents had been excluded from royal service.

Hessa watched from a bench because the quay shifted whenever she stood.

No oathwake reached her from the failed seal. She felt a dull pressure behind her left ear, then nothing. A week earlier, such silence would have meant Hush. Now it might mean release, hearing loss, ordinary distance, or a bad angle between water and stone.

Her body no longer told her which.

A Saltjaw clerk offered his arm. Hessa took it and rose. Along the quay, every abolished privilege had become a practical problem. Royal rescue stations still owned boats but no compelled crews. Tax currents no longer carried treasury barges ahead of local vessels. Three island delegations demanded pilots' fees from Merathi grain captains. A harbor council claimed emergency control until new rules could be voted. Fishers asked whether that language merely rebuilt a crown with shorter robes.

Nobody had drowned yet.

That counted as a successful morning.

At the customs house, Nimm's supply tables had replaced the royal tide board. White tabs marked water routes. Blue tabs showed grain. Open hands indicated rescue priority. The system worked because crews kept updating it, not because the sea enforced the chalk.

Hessa entered the failure of the old harbor seal under her own uncertain hearing. Beside it she wrote the names of all twelve witnesses and the clerk's objection that physical access still required central coordination.

Evidence had survived the crowns.

So had administration, hunger, self-interest, and tides.

Varo relinquished emergency rule before noon and refused to ask forgiveness.

The proceeding took place on a repaired Kett service dock moored in Merathi water but detached from Merathi land. No polity accepted another's courthouse. The dock held four custody groups: Dalen's Merathi guards, Ironwake witnesses under Keth, Saltjaw clerks under Orra, and Kett labor marshals chosen by the hull councils.

Varo wore no Tide Chancellor's seal. His coat still showed the stitches where one had hung.

A crowd filled boats around the dock. Heren Bay survivors held blue cloth strips. Forge workers carried masks stained by coral powder. Families from erased Ironwake settlements raised clay tablets. Hush sailors occupied two barges, divided between those seeking amnesty and those demanding recognition that their work had prevented earlier storms.

Hessa sat at a plain table with three other temporary conveners. A Veyran island claimant sat to her left. A Merathi dock magistrate took the right. Across from them, an Ironwake advocate arranged charge tablets in seven stacks.

“No bench may decide final jurisdiction today,” Hessa said. “This hearing records custody, admissions, contested charges, and immediate protections. Its authority expires at sunset.”

Keth objected. “Varo may leave Merathi custody at sunset.”

Dalen answered, “He remains under my arrest by the naval code.”

“The same code he ruled by decree.”

“Yes.”

“That is not custody. It is succession.”

The Merathi magistrate struck the table. “Ironwake has no right to remove a Merathi citizen into hull law.”

An Ironwake woman in the crowd held up a tablet bearing her mother's erased name. “His glass came into our dead. That creates standing.”

Voices multiplied. Hessa could not hear magical pressure under them. She could hear anger, fear, practiced argument, and several people who had not eaten.

She called a food interval before asking Varo to speak.

When proceedings resumed, he stood without counsel.

“I relinquish every emergency office, decree authority, fleet privilege, and seal held under the Hush mandate,” he said. “I accept continued custody. I do not admit that establishing Hush constituted a crime. I admit authorizing witness-coral extraction, secret detentions at three naval yards, seizure of Kett service docks, attacks against coalition shipping, and execution of eleven officers after closed emergency courts.”

The crowd broke around the number.

Eleven names entered the record. Varo supplied them from memory. He also named detention yards, array accounts, surviving administrators, and four archives likely to contain falsified reef surveys.

A Heren Bay survivor shouted, “Do not use our dead to purchase honesty now.”

“I am not,” Varo said.

“You used them to purchase eleven years.”

“Yes.”

That answer caused more fury than denial might have done. Hessa let it remain in the room.

The public judgment could not conclude in one session. Ironwake demanded transfer to a multi-polity court. Merathi dock councils demanded local trial for executions and unlawful seizure. Forge workers wanted labor deaths heard before naval strategy. Heren Bay families split: some called Varo a murderer who had made their grief imperial; others feared that condemning Hush would return coastal safety to people who had ignored them before.

Orra proposed divided charges with a single custody compact. Keth rejected any compact giving Saltjaw judges a permanent role. Dalen accepted outside observation but not removal. Kett marshals offered the most practical answer: Varo could remain on a movable dock, guarded by rotating groups, with no group holding both keys to his cabin.

Varo objected once. “Do not call a cabin a cell if guards can move it into open sea.”

Nimm, listening from the rail with bandaged hands, replied, “Then we bolt it to Hull Six and post the tow routes.”

The temporary conveners adopted Kett custody for thirty days, renewable only by a fresh public hearing. Merath retained legal objection. Ironwake retained transfer claim. Varo's admissions entered witness coral without granting his titles any force.

They also entered protections for witnesses who had served Hush. An Ironwake advocate objected that anonymity would reproduce hidden rule. A forge worker answered that full public names could put families under attack before testimony began. The compromise sealed home locations while preserving witness names, roles, and acts. Any court seeking the locations would need a separate panel and a stated protective purpose.

Dalen filed the altered-array ledger. She admitted helping Varo conceal the outward route and listed every engineer who had known after ignition. Two had wanted to close it. One had attempted to warn Rusk. Three had kept the duct open while coalition fire struck the platform. Their conduct would not fit one category of loyalist or defector.

Varo requested that responsibility for the false ledger remain his. Dalen refused. “You ordered the concealment. I maintained it after I knew what it did. The record can carry two decisions.”

Hessa watched him accept that correction. His willingness did not reduce the closed courts or restore powdered names. It did place an accurate join in evidence where a ruler's solitary confession might otherwise absorb everyone else's agency.

When the guards led him away, the crowd did not agree whether justice had begun.

It had acquired a schedule.

Reparations began with things people could lift.

Hush stores held nine hundred and twelve intact glass fittings, forty-three sealed powder casks, eleven naval charts built from erased reef memory, six captured merchant hulls, and grain purchased under emergency levy. Every item carried more than one claim.

Forge workers wanted glass destroyed. Nimm argued that outward-facing fittings could route names during local release hearings. Veyran gardeners warned that even sealed glass disoriented fish. Keth demanded Ironwake custody over casks containing massacre dust. Merathi harbor councils claimed naval matériel as public property needed for defense.

Hessa required separate tables.

On the first, physical origin. On the second, harms caused. On the third, current necessity. On the fourth, proposed use and expiry. No table decided the others.

The six merchant hulls returned fastest. Four owners remained alive and could document seizure. One hull belonged to a dissolved company whose creditors included Heren Bay families and Kett guilds. The last had been taken from an unnamed island that refused to state its harbor before water.

A Merathi registrar insisted property could not transfer without a registered destination.

Sivren, present with bark charts rolled under one arm, said, “Then your form has outlived its command.”

They gave the islanders the ship in open water. The record described consented bearings but omitted the homeward turn.

Grain produced harder choices. Original levies had come from thirty-one districts. Returning exact sacks would starve refugee kitchens using them now. The districts appointed claimants. Refugee councils named reliance. Hessa helped negotiate staged replacement from the next three harvests, subject to weather review. Seven districts refused and filed seizure claims instead.

Nobody crossed out their refusal.

The coral powder casks stayed sealed. Ironwake witnesses and forge workers took joint keys; Veyran gardeners monitored leakage; Merathi engineers retained access only under observation. Keth called the arrangement too weak. Eshara called it dangerously close to permanent storage. Nimm called it the least bad box available before lunch.

Ship repair raised another debt. Hush yards contained timber cut under levy from Blue Keel islands, iron purchased from Ironwake guilds through intermediaries, and root resin harvested from Veyran labor camps. Returning raw material would strand damaged civilian vessels in Merathi ports. Using it without compensation would continue the taking.

The dock councils issued repair shares instead. Half the seized stock went immediately to civilian and rescue hulls regardless of former allegiance. Source communities received claims against future yard production, published by quantity and due date. Blue Keel accepted timber shares but rejected Merathi valuation. Ironwake demanded physical iron first. Two Veyran camps denied the authority of representatives who had signed for resin on their behalf.

Hessa wrote every refusal beside the allocated stock. A schedule could begin repayment. It could also disguise delay until witnesses stopped looking. The traveling hearings would have to return before each due date and ask what had actually moved.

Reef grounds required maps. Sivren would not make an authoritative one. She laid translucent sheets over one another: guild contracts, island testimony, fish migration, former crown claims, storm damage, coral death. The overlaps showed where reparations could restore access and where returning one claim would dispossess another living community.

Hessa looked at the layered water and felt no hidden verdict.

Good.

At midday, the first restitution boats left carrying three coral cradles toward Leth Avar, grain to two stripped districts, and copies of forge labor records to families whose dead had been listed only as material loss. Other goods stayed under dispute.

Reparations had begun. Completion lay nowhere near the harbor.

The offer of supreme judgeship arrived in a copper tube sealed by eleven polities and rejected by six before Hessa opened it.

Talren watched from a shaded chair, his right arm immobilized from shoulder to wrist. Sivren stood beside an unrooted chart table. Nimm occupied the floor because sitting low kept array burns above their heart. Keth and Eshara had come to oppose the proposal for different reasons. Orra had helped draft it and already disliked its title.

The document asked Hessa to serve as High Wake Convener for seven years, empowered to certify standing, supervise release hearings, and resolve disputes among successor courts.

“We removed hereditary command,” Keth said. “Now eleven governments wish to replace it with an orc they find temporarily admirable.”

“Not because she is an orc,” said the Merathi delegate.

“That does not improve your sentence.”

Eshara touched the copper tube. “A central archive gathers authority even when its keeper intends humility.”

Orra said, “Coasts need hearings. Silence will favor whoever already owns ships and records.”

Talren shifted against pain. “They need coordination too. My command circles worked because routes and signals existed before refusal. Refusal without shared forms becomes collision.”

All looked to Hessa.

She had wanted such a seat once, though not under this title. After certifying the Nine-Harbor oath, she had believed better hearing could protect people from secret clauses. The belief contained truth. It had also allowed other witnesses to surrender judgment into her body.

“My hearing is failing,” she said.

The Merathi delegate answered too quickly. “Assistants can extend it.”

“That is your argument for why I should own authority?”

“No. I mean the office need not depend on one body.”

“Then it does not need my office.”

Hessa tore the seven-year mandate across the line granting final certification.

Orra winced. “We worked on that wording.”

“Keep the parts that work.”

They drafted again with charcoal on the table.

Traveling hearings would carry no permanent supreme judge. Each mandate named a route, subject, participating courts, custody limits, and an expiry no longer than one season. Local communities could name standing and refuse the hearing, though refusal entered the public record. Conveners rotated across peoples and professions. No person who held evidence custody could also control the final record. Witness coral preserved names and disagreement but issued no sentence. Appeals moved to a new panel rather than upward to a fixed throne.

Keth insisted military confederacies retain withdrawal from any panel claiming territorial authority. Eshara demanded archive copies return to source communities. Nimm required published stores and travel costs so poor islands could see whether a hearing would consume their winter grain. Talren added common rescue and navigation signals.

The Merathi delegate objected that seasonal expiry would let complex cases lapse.

“Renew them with living consent,” Hessa said.

“Parties may refuse renewal to escape judgment.”

“Then custody protections and filed claims continue under separate law. Do not make every useful thing permanent because one danger exists.”

They produced no universal charter. Saltjaw accepted the first three routes. Ironwake agreed to one hearing concerning powder casks and rejected two others. Sen Veyra's sap-cutters joined while two Root-Mothers withheld archive access. Three Merathi harbor councils signed. The imperial interior had sent no representatives.

The first mandate covered Khor, Rainward, and Heren Bay. It would expire in ninety days.

Hessa signed as one convener among five.

Her first hearing without reliable oathwake concerned two pilots and a widow.

The widow, Mar Sorel, claimed both pilots had abandoned her husband's grain ship during the crownstorm passage. Pilot Renn said the ship had refused a safe bearing. Pilot Avra said no safe bearing existed after the royal road surfaced. Under old practice, Hessa would have listened to water held in a court basin and identified pressure around the public rescue promise.

She heard only a faint rushing.

Panic came quietly. Without magic, every pause looked like failure. The widow watched Hessa's throat plates, waiting for certainty. The pilots watched too.

Hessa set her staff flat upon the floor.

“My hearing loss prevents me from certifying oathwake here,” she said. “We will proceed through living testimony and material record. Any party may request another wake-hearer.”

Mar Sorel's face tightened. “Then why are you sitting there?”

“A useful question.”

Hessa offered to withdraw. The widow considered and told her to remain, provided Orra examined the basin later.

They began with the damaged ship's log. It showed a bearing received from Sivren's distributed map, then crossed out. Renn claimed the captain rejected it because a Saltjaw source had supplied the pressure reading. Avra remembered the captain accepting, then turning when Blue Keel signaled hidden stone.

Their accounts conflicted.

A deck child had drawn route tabs during the crossing. Her slate showed both signals arriving within the same minute. The captain had ordered east before the Blue Keel warning, then reversed too late. Neither pilot had possessed authority to override him. Renn had left in a skiff after the hull struck. Avra had stayed and rescued five people but not Mar Sorel's husband.

The widow asked why Renn took the skiff.

“Because the grain shifted and I thought the ship would roll,” Renn said.

“You saved yourself.”

“Yes.”

“Did you call anyone?”

“No.”

That admission mattered. No sea pressure announced its weight. Hessa had to watch Mar Sorel receive it and Renn endure having spoken it.

Initial testimony suggested abandonment by Renn and no breach by Avra. Then a kitchen worker from Kett stepped forward. She had seen Renn return twice in the skiff after the roll. His second trip pulled three children from a sail tangle. Renn had omitted this because he believed rescue after fleeing did not excuse the first act.

Mar Sorel began to cry. “My husband put those children in the sail cradle.”

The facts changed without becoming kinder.

Hessa adjusted the proposed finding. Renn had abandoned the deck without warning, then returned under his own choice and saved three. Avra had remained. The captain's route prejudice contributed to the wreck. Mar Sorel's husband died after helping children reach recoverable shelter. Damages and recognition would divide accordingly, subject to the widow's claim against the captain's estate.

No magical command settled acceptance. Mar Sorel disputed the reduction in Renn's liability. Renn disputed any credit for returning. Avra demanded the report name the delayed Blue Keel signal. All three objections entered the record.

When the hearing ended, Orra asked whether Hessa missed the old certainty.

“Every breath,” Hessa said.

Outside, the failed royal harbor seal lay on the quay beside coils of ordinary rope. Pilots shouted depths to incoming boats. Witness coral in a low basin held the day's names and issued no instructions.

Five conveners boarded a patched cutter carrying the first ninety-day mandate. Hessa stepped aboard last, accepted a sailor's arm, and listened while living witnesses argued over the next port.

Chapter 28 — Harbors Without Crowns

One year later, Sivren failed three students for drawing the same correct route.

They stood around a tide table in Sen Veyra's new navigation house, each hand map showing an elegant green curve through the eastern roots. Outside, monsoon water drummed upon leaf roofs. A dozen practice skiffs waited in the channel below.

“The route is open,” said the youngest student.

“Yes.”

“The soundings agree.”

“At low tide.”

“We marked the tide.”

Sivren laid the three sheets atop one another. Every line vanished under the others. “Where did your reports come from?”

The students pointed to the same archive root.

That root had learned the route from fishers, sap cutters, two former imperial pilots, and a free-island trader who allowed bearings copied only for one season. The students had flattened those sources into archive certainty.

“Go ask again,” Sivren said. “Bring me a disagreement you can locate.”

One student groaned. Another reached for the maps.

“Leave these,” Sivren told them. “Error needs an archive too.”

They departed in three skiffs, choosing different channels after a brief, encouraging argument.

The navigation house had no root-throne. Living mangrove tendrils entered through every wall, each attached to a source ledger and a date after which its report required renewal. Maps hung in layers rather than as final surfaces. Students learned route craft, politics of access, hull draft, weather, witness protocol, and the expense of sending someone to ask whether an old permission still held.

Admission had caused the first serious fight. Root courts wanted apprentices chosen through archive families. Island councils demanded reserved places. Kett offered to fund poor students if the school taught modular-hull navigation. Sivren accepted funds with conditions published on the wall, then watched wealthier families pay pilots to coach their children before entrance tests.

The school changed the test. Applicants could bring a route learned in work, migration, fishing, or rescue instead of reading a formal root chart. Fees moved to a sliding grain measure reviewed each season. This admitted more working pilots and angered archive scholars who had spent decades mastering notation. Sivren gave those scholars teaching positions only if they could explain why notation served people who did not use it.

One failed and left. Two stayed. The resulting arguments improved the first-year charts.

A memory-repair room occupied the smallest wing. Students who rooted maps could record what they expected to lose and choose whether any archive copy should return later. No ceremony called the cost noble. One apprentice had sacrificed the taste of her father's soup and regretted it so fiercely she abandoned root work. The school kept her as a current reader. Expertise did not require repeating Sivren's wounds.

Two Root-Mothers had denounced the school as a market stall for uncertified memory. Sap-cutter councils called its fees too high. Island pilots accused Sivren of turning consent into another form. All three criticisms had entered the entrance wall in waterproof ink.

Eshara taught there twice each month.

The Root-Mother arrived carrying no attendants and three disputed archive casks. Since burning the property roots, she had lost thousands of copied boundaries and retained the institutional habits that created them. Some days she defended an archive practice before remembering she meant to question it.

This morning she opened a cask from Leth Avar. Inside, three witness-coral cuttings had grown around the restored bell's note.

“They chose sites,” Eshara said. “One at their present harbor. One on the drowned shelf. One travels with their rescue boats.”

“Did they accept gardeners?”

“One from Sen Veyra. Two trained locally. They rejected my first list.”

Sivren smiled.

“That response grows less charming with repetition,” Eshara said.

“You taught me durability.”

“I taught you obedience and called it continuity.”

The statement carried no request for comfort. Sivren helped unload the cask.

Her left hand still numbed in cold rain after the crownstorm passage. Bark tracery around the wrist had grown pale where severed roots took the memory of a childhood summer. More absence waited behind it: her mother's face, surrendered when Sivren burned the authoritative maps. She retained facts. Her mother had worn copper bells at one wrist. She sang while cutting mangrove rot. She disliked sweet wine and made a clicking sound before contradicting a Root-Mother.

No features came.

Sivren had stopped trying to manufacture them from other relatives.

She drew a bell beside the Leth Avar cutting and wrote the source in her mother's old ink recipe. Love could persist without a recovered picture. So could grief.

A Kett courier arrived at noon with a route tab marked open hand.

Walking Harbor meant to reconnect.

Kett had spent a year discovering that separation could become another habit of fear.

The fourteen harbor hulls waited across a broad bay west of Sen Veyra. Repairs showed in mismatched planks. Hull Four still lacked the outer deck sacrificed at Hollow Meridian. Hull Six carried the movable hearing chamber once used for Varo's custody. Children had painted fish around its barred windows after the chamber moved ashore.

Nimm stood on a joining bridge with a measuring rod between their bandaged palms. Array burns had stiffened two fingers of the right hand. They compensated by changing grip and refusing assistance unless a bolt required both hands.

“No ceremony until the pins take load,” they told the gathered councils.

A merchant guild had proposed reconnecting all fourteen hulls under the old central harbor charter. Refugee councils objected that the charter gave original residents priority over berths. Labor councils wanted guild property claims suspended until the public inquiry ended. Three hulls preferred permanent independence. Hull Nine offered docking only during storm season.

Kett therefore joined by pairs.

Hull Two accepted Hull Six for a ninety-day grain and medical compact. Hull Four docked with Hull Nine around a shared mast workshop. Hull Eleven, carrying many siege survivors, linked to no one but opened two pedestrian bridges during daylight. Every join had load limits, resource terms, exit procedures, and a date for renewal.

Sivren stood beside Nimm as the first pin entered.

“Your students still draw roots as if they cannot move?” Nimm asked.

“They have advanced to drawing currents as if they cannot lie.”

“Education.”

The capstan turned. Goblin dock crews worked beside human refugees, orc caulkers, and two Veyran bridge growers whose root clamps had offended every traditional Kett carpenter. The pin slid through three iron collars.

No oathwake steadied it.

Workers checked strain by plumb line and sound. A woman below struck the joining beam with a mallet. Nimm listened, ordered one collar shimmed, and tested again.

Only then did the councils cross.

The supply web had become ordinary infrastructure. Its colored routes now carried grain, medicine, schoolbooks, fish spawn, court records, and letters across harbors that no longer received privileged currents. Captains still used black-square withdrawal and open-hand rescue. Some merchants abused rescue marks to enter crowded docks. Kett fined them in rope and labor rather than trusting magic to know intent.

Guild trials continued. Nimm had testified against three former masters and for two workers accused of sabotaging Hush keels. The Barrow Guild kept its name after a vote Nimm lost. It had lost exclusive inspection authority after a vote they won.

“What happened to the hidden fitting?” Sivren asked.

“Turned outward at a local hearing near Blue Keel. Worked twice. Cracked on the third use. Now it sits in a glass school where children are instructed not to believe adults who label a box safe.”

Beyond the joined hulls, a training carrack shortened sail.

Talren Dask stood on its quarterdeck.

Talren trained command by giving students attractive orders with concealed defects.

Today's order sent three practice vessels through a narrow cut on a rising tide. The route saved two hours and put the deepest hull within one handspan of stone. Talren supplied accurate soundings but omitted that a fishing nursery had been marked there after the old tax current ended.

Two trainees accepted. The third raised a split-circle refusal.

Sivren watched from a Kett launch as Talren signaled for cause.

Living stakes in cut. Alternate north costs two hours.

Talren accepted the refusal and asked the other crews whether they changed course. One did. The lead trainee continued, arguing her shallow vessel would not touch the stakes.

A year earlier, instructors might have praised decisive command. Talren let the trainee complete the cut. At the far end, fishers waited with a damage claim for wake disturbance. No hull had struck anything. The vessel still owed compensation for entering a marked nursery at unsafe speed.

“Refusal saved the third crew from the claim,” Talren told the class. “It did not make the refusing captain correct about every future route. Nor did my bad order make all orders suspect. Record conditions. Change what changes.”

His right shoulder no longer lifted above his lowest rib. He wore no sling, but a cadet handled the heavy signal rack. Some mornings pain shortened his temper. Crew knew they could call a pause without punishment. Two officers had left his school because they considered that practice an invitation to indiscipline.

Mothwake rode at anchor beyond the cut with a replacement mast and a darker square where the Hush bolt had entered. The rope-store sailor, now using wheeled braces built by Kett, taught collision procedure. The carpenter who had stayed behind the bulkhead refused any heroic version of the story and corrected Talren whenever he omitted that she had been terrified.

Sivren joined him after the exercise.

“Khor sent the current notice,” Talren said.

“So did three students.”

“Same route?”

“Not anymore.”

He looked toward the trainees arguing with fishers over compensation. “Good.”

A Saltjaw courier brought news from the traveling hearings. Varo's final trial panel had issued judgment after nine months, four venue changes, and two custody challenges. He had been convicted for the eleven closed-court executions, unlawful detention, seizure of Kett docks, and extraction of witness coral without standing. The panel rejected Ironwake's charge that every act of Hush engineering constituted massacre because evidence showed some early arrays prevented immediate storm deaths before the program expanded into erasure.

No faction received the whole judgment it wanted.

Varo had received twenty years in open-record custody, reviewable after ten but never convertible into office. He lived at a repaired Heren Bay station under mixed guard, cataloguing Hush accounts and answering claimant questions. Families could refuse contact. His technical testimony had already located two coral burial sites. It had not reduced his sentence.

Keth called the review provision an escape hatch. Some Heren Bay families called twenty years vengeance against the person who had saved later coasts. Kett claimants wanted priority over any future review. Hessa's panel entered all objections and expired.

“Do you agree with it?” Sivren asked.

Talren watched the water. “I agree they wrote reasons and a date to look again. Ask me after twenty years whether that was enough.”

They sailed toward Khor under three different charts.

Orc politics had grown louder without royal currents pushing every hull toward inherited agreements.

Saltjaw courts established release hearings in six shore cities. Ironwake refused two of their judgments and created its own massacre-record panels. Blue Keel withdrew from a Seven Hull trade conference after larger confederacies tried to standardize withdrawal signals into binding naval code. Keth remained an admiral, allied with Hessa on coral custody and opposed to her on jurisdiction.

Orra renewed three ancestral obligations in her congregations and released nine. She required every renewal to name a living term. Traditionalists accused her of teaching descendants to bargain with ancestors. Reformers accused her of preserving commands through reverence. She considered both accusations useful.

Elf factions divided too. Sen Veyra returned seven island-name archives and faced twelve rival claims over them. Some islanders wanted records destroyed. Others wanted copies without root-court custody. Archive workers formed a council that opposed both Eshara's former sovereignty and Sivren's dependence on traveling source testimony, which they argued favored communities wealthy enough to send pilots.

Sivren agreed with the criticism and disliked its solution of centralized subsidy control.

Politics had not become clean. It had become harder to pretend disagreement came from ancient commands rather than present interests.

At Rainward, repaired salt towns charged coalition veterans harbor fees and granted Hush survivors no exemption. At Merath, three dock republics competed with an inland provisional assembly that still called itself the Crown Administration despite possessing no crown authority over water. Free islands opened routes and closed others. Merchant insurers raised prices on unstandardized charts. Fish migrations began recovering around dead glass zones, though two reefs remained silent.

Reparations moved unevenly. Fourteen grain districts had received full replacement. Nine remained on schedule. Eight had filed claims after rejecting staged return. Ironwake recovered two sealed powder casks and discovered that one contained mixed names from three coasts, preventing simple territorial custody. Leth Avar's rescue boats answered seven calls and refused an eighth because the requesting merchant had invoked the old protection clause rather than asking living people.

None of this belonged in a triumphant map.

Sivren entered it anyway.

Khor's low-tide court had been rebuilt three steps higher and left the lowest basalt platform broken.

At ebb, the old treaty stair still showed through shallow water. It no longer drove storms through the court. Children used its top stones to launch toy boats until clerks chased them away from active hearings.

Hessa waited beside a basin of witness coral.

She leaned harder on the weighted staff than she had a year before. Her left ear carried no oathwake. The right caught only strong patterns at close range, enough to recognize pressure and not enough to certify wording. Five younger hearers worked in rotation around the court. Hessa served as one convener when mandates brought her home and spent more time asking who had not spoken.

“You came late,” she told Sivren.

“Kett reconnected.”

“All of it?”

“Enough to argue across bridges.”

Talren joined them. Nimm arrived the next morning aboard a supply launch carrying three kinds of rope and no ceremonial wine. Keth had sent an Ironwake observer rather than come under a Saltjaw court mandate. Eshara remained in Sen Veyra for an archive-workers' strike.

The hearing at Khor concerned the unnamed current.

It had appeared after the release, running north of the old Nine-Harbor route. Three islands used it for trade. A Merathi dock republic wanted standardized charts. The nearest free island consented to bearings through one season but refused to name the current after any polity, navigator, battle, or dead ruler. Fishers from Khor argued that leaving it unnamed made rescue calls dangerous.

A child sat among the source witnesses with a slate across her knees.

Her name was Pera. She had traveled the current six times with her aunts and drawn every passage differently. One line followed depth. Another showed birds. A third mapped where crews had disagreed about turning home.

The Merathi delegate asked which line could enter official charts.

“All,” Pera said.

“That is not a navigable answer.”

“We navigated.”

Sivren concealed a smile.

Hessa asked the free-island witnesses whether seasonal use of a descriptive signal would violate their refusal to name. They accepted north-opening current as a temporary call phrase, provided charts recorded its expiry and no capital letters turned description into title. Khor fishers accepted that for rescue boards and reserved a demand for permanent safety notation.

Nobody named the current.

At sunset, coral branches in the basin brightened. Names spoken during repair traveled through them: people denied salt landings under the annex, Ironwake dead recovered from powder, Leth Avar families, Heren Bay rescuers, Kett civilians killed in the siege, Hush workers burned by glass, sailors from both fleets lost at Hollow Meridian.

Corrections interrupted the recital. A Kett woman objected that her brother's craft title had been entered as guildmaster when he had died a striking worker. A Merathi family asked that their daughter's service aboard Hush remain in the record beside her later rescue of coalition sailors. Two Ironwake witnesses disputed the pronunciation of a massacre name preserved from damaged coral. Hessa stopped the reading each time.

No correction erased the earlier form. Clerks attached a dated amendment, named its source, and asked whether anyone contested it. The process made the recital longer and less graceful. People shifted on wet stone. Children grew hungry. Nimm opened a biscuit box near the witness table and earned a glare from a court clerk until Hessa asked for one.

Sivren thought of old root ceremonies where a clean collective voice had concealed edits already made below the chamber. This untidy record showed its joins. A future hearing might change them again.

The witness coral spoke names without orders.

No voice commanded a ship. No pressure forced a descendant into service. Those present could answer, grieve, dispute pronunciation, request correction, or walk away.

Sivren heard her mother's name among a Veyran list contributed by Eshara. Not because her mother had suffered a famous harm. She had witnessed three islanders contest a property map and preserved their objection in a private cutting when the root court excluded it.

Sivren still could not see her face.

Pera looked up from the slate. “Did I draw the current wrong?”

The child's newest chart showed four lines. One ended at Khor. One curved toward Kett. One vanished at the page edge. The last returned to its beginning under a question mark.

“Which did you travel?” Sivren asked.

“The first two.”

“And the others?”

“Possible.”

“Then mark who thinks so, and when.”

Pera wrote her own name beside one line. Beside the other she wrote unknown after winter.

The tide rose over the broken court step. Witness coral continued speaking as water covered it, names moving through the reef without pulling any chart closed.
